### 1. ★★★★☆ 'Time for Ukraine to get new president,' says Trump after Zelensky condemns diesel deal

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business / GN Geopolitics / GN Top / DW World
- **多源确认**: 8
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T01:41:29+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Figure caption, Watch: &#x27;Time for Ukraine to get a new president&#x27;, says Trump
US President Donald Trump has said he thinks "it&#x27;s time for Ukraine to get a new president" as he responded to Volodymyr Zelensky&#x27;s criticism of the US diesel deal with Russia.
The Ukrainian president warned a new deal to release millions of tonnes of Russian diesel into the US would provide Moscow with money to continue its war against his nation, calling it a gift to Russian President Vladimir Putin.
Trump told reporters outside the White House on Saturday that Zelensky "wants to make problems for the world" by continuing to authorise attacks on Russian refineries.
"He&#x27;d better damn well stop," Trump said, before suggesting Ukraine should "get a new leader who can make a deal".
Since Russia invaded Ukraine in 2022 the country has been under martial law, meaning elections are suspended.
Global fuel supplies, including diesel, have been severely limited by the war against Iran launched by the US and Israel, which led to the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz, through which roughly a fifth of the world&#x27;s oil products usually flows.
Russia - a major producer - and Ukraine have recently intensified their strikes on each other&#x27;s energy infrastructure and transport facilities, further straining energy markets.
"We said, &#x27;you can do whatever you want to Russia, don&#x27;t hit the refineries&#x27;, because that&#x27;s a world problem that&#x27;s caused," Trump continued.
"He could have made many deals and for some reason, he never does."
Putin&#x27;s envoy Kirill Dmitriev described Trump&#x27;s intervention as "iconic and historic", saying that Ukraine "needs leadership actually focused on peace".
The US president confirmed on Friday that an agreement had been reached with Moscow to suspend sanctions on Russian diesel exports until 7 April.
Zelensky reacted by saying it would allow Moscow to raise new funds to source new military equipment to continue the war.
He also accused Moscow of taking the deal "to mean they can keep fighting, to mean they keep dropping bombs on cities" following deadly new strikes on Zaporizhzhia.
Analysis: Trump&#x27;s shock Russia deal highlights mounting pressure to curb fuel prices
The Ukrainian president spoke to several allies on Saturday to discuss the latest developments, calling for "real decisions to protect lives in Ukraine" and to protect European security.
A Downing Street spokesperson said UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham had spoken to Zelensky and offered his "complete solidarity".
The pair had agreed Russia should acquiesce to "an immediate energy ceasefire" and end its attacks on shipping in the Black Sea, the spokesperson said, as "this would immediately release more energy and food supplies into the global market".
News of the US-Russia diesel deal was also met with criticism by EU&#x27;s foreign affairs chief Kaja Kallas, who echoed Zelensky in saying that suspending sanctions "provides Moscow with more revenues to wage war", adding: "This is not the time to ease pressure on Russia, and Europe won&#x27;t."
She said European foreign ministers intended to approve the biggest set of sanctions on Russia since the start of its full-scale invasion on Monday.
The deal with Russia is the latest effort from Trump to lower fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterm elections, after months spent grappling with the political consequences of the Iran war.
That conflict, which began in February, has sent the cost of petrol and in particular diesel skyrocketing, which has soured his standing with the American public who have been stung by the higher pump prices and knock-on effects that led to across-the-board inflation.
The average price of diesel is currently $6.28 (£4.74) a gallon, according to the AAA – up from last month&#x27;s $5.94 average, and $3.68 last year.
Under the deal, Russia would release an initial 300,000 tonnes of diesel "to the American and global marketplace" followed by an additional 500,000 tonnes in November.
A further million would follow, Trump wrote on Truth Social, and then another 3m tonnes "within a short period of time" – but he noted that this delivery will be "based on the condition of their diesel refineries".
In the past 10 months, Russia experienced two waves of severe fuel shortages across the country due to Ukrainian drones strikes on its oil refineries.
Diesel production in Russia dropped by an estimated 30%, according to the International Energy Agency.
Trump had already called for new elections in Ukraine last December , external , saying Ukrainian voters should have the choice to replace Zelensky.
Since the war broke out in 2022 Ukraine has been under martial law, meaning elections are suspended.
Trump announces deal for Russian diesel as Zelensky calls it a gift to Putin
Russian glide bomb attack on Zaporizhzhia kills at least 15 people

### 2. ★★★★☆ US military says it disabled cargo vessel trying to run Iran blockade - Reuters

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Reuters World / BBC World / GN Geopolitics / GN Energy / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 5
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T01:35:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, The row over the White House pool affected live TV coverage of Trump events last month
The US justice department (DoJ) has said it is investigating several TV networks over whether they broke federal competition laws when they recently suspended pool coverage of President Donald Trump.
Last month, five networks paused their presidential coverage for several days in response to the Trump administration withdrawing the credentials of reporters from CNN, MS Now and Politico.
The justice department asked ABC, CBS, CNN, NBC and Fox News to hand over communications related to the pool and said that the boycott could violate the Sherman Act, referring to a law that bars companies from engaging in monopolistic business practices.
Pool members usually share their material with the wider media.
A DoJ spokesperson said: "The Antitrust Division is simply investigating to determine whether these ​news organisations have violated the antitrust laws."
Trump blocked CNN, MS Now and Politico from the White House last month after accusing the organisations of writing "fiction or lies" about his team.
The White House later reinstated their access after a ruling from a judge. The three outlets had sued the Trump administration, arguing the restricted access violated their First Amendment rights.
The First Amendment forbids the government from "abridging" the freedom of the press - language that courts have traditionally interpreted as preventing the government from discriminating against the media based on the content of their coverage.
The Trump administration has taken a number of legal actions against news publications in the US, including lawsuits over coverage of the president.
In February, the White House announced it was taking control of the press pool which has access to Trump and had for more than a century been managed by the White House Correspondents&#x27; Association.
The same month, the Trump administration moved to bar Associated Press (AP) reporters and photographers from spaces where access is limited, such as the Oval Office or Air Force One, because of the outlet&#x27;s usage of the term "Gulf of Mexico" instead of "Gulf of America". AP filed a lawsuit and the case remains ongoing.
CNN, MS NOW and Politico file lawsuit against Trump&#x27;s White House ban
Major US outlets suspend shared Trump coverage in protest at media ban

### 5. ★★★☆☆ The midterm strategy driving Democrats deep into Trump country - Reuters

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: Reuters World / AP World / BBC World / GN Fed / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 7
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:28:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US death row inmate Christa Pike has been discharged from hospital following her botched execution at the end of last month, her lawyers have said.
Pike, 50, has been sent back to the prison where she has spent the last 30 years and is continuing to receive medical care, according to a statement from her lawyers on Saturday.
She survived two lethal injections of the drug pentobarbital on 30 September, which left her in a critical condition.
Pike regained consciousness earlier this week and began speaking - stunning experts, medical staff and her lawyers.
Her case is now in uncharted territory as she is thought to be the only person to have survived an execution attempt by lethal injection.
I&#x27;ve seen nearly 500 executions - but never one like Christa Pike&#x27;s
I interviewed Christa Pike 20 years ago - I can&#x27;t stop thinking about her
What happened in Christa Pike&#x27;s failed US execution, and how did she survive?
Pike&#x27;s lawyers said in their statement that she was "grateful to the first responders and her medical team for their care in these extraordinary circumstances".
It is unclear what medical care Pike is receiving now that she is back in prison but her lawyers previously said she faced a "long road to recovery".
The Tennessee Department of Correction is yet to say why the execution did not go as planned, though it has defended the method it used.
Figure caption, How widely used is the death penalty in the US?
At a court hearing on Wednesday, Tennessee Assistant Attorney General Will Ayers told the judge he did not know whether the state intends to try to execute her again.
The judge ordered the state to preserve all evidence related to the failed injections. That evidence could lay the groundwork for a future lawsuit against the state.
Pike was 18 when she and her then-boyfriend, Tadaryl Shipp, beat, tortured and killed 19-year-old Colleen Slemmer, whom they had met at a job training camp for low-income young people.
Shipp was spared the death penalty because he was not legally an adult when the crime was committed. He remains in prison.
Pike was the first woman scheduled to be executed in the state of Tennessee in over 200 years.
Her lawyers have spent decades appealing her case. In the days leading up to her scheduled execution, both the US Supreme Court and Tennessee Governor Bill Lee declined to block the execution.
Since it failed, Pike&#x27;s lawyers have renewed their calls for her sentence to be commuted to life in prison without the possibility of parole.
Image caption, Colleen Slemmer was tortured and brutally killed by Pike and Shipp
Pike lost consciousness and was taken to a local hospital following her botched execution. Her legal team believed for days that she was brain dead and expected her to be taken off life support at any moment.
Pike eventually woke up and began talking again on Tuesday - her lawyer Randy Spivey said her first words were "Where am I?" and "Who are you?"
At a press conference on Wednesday, her lawyers said she was shackled to the bed and suffering from pneumonia and significant swelling on her arms, while also being unable to swallow.
"We don&#x27;t know the toll all of this will take" Spivey said of her mental health, adding that Pike felt "angry and confused".
Christa Pike now walking after failed US execution, lawyer tells BBC
Christa Pike has &#x27;profound&#x27; health turnaround after botched execution, lawyers say

### 6. ★★★☆☆ South Korea completes Arctic-Europe commercial voyage, eyes regular service despite risks - Reuters

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Reuters World / GN AI
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T01:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: bne IntelliNews - South Korea's Samsung expects record profit as AI chip demand surges
South Korea's Samsung expects record profit as AI chip demand surges
Samsung Electronics (KRX: 005930) expects third-quarter operating profit to have jumped 782.5% year on year to KRW107.4 trillion ($80.1bn), as artificial intelligence drives a worldwide memory chip shortage and pushes up prices, AFP reports.
The South Korean group is the world's biggest memory chipmaker, and data centres running AI are buying up its advanced products. The preliminary figure would make Samsung the first South Korean company to earn more than KRW100 trillion in operating profit in a single quarter. It would also extend the company's run of record sales and profit to four straight quarters.
Revenue for July to September probably climbed 127% to KRW195 trillion, the company said in a statement. Final results are due at the end of October.
South Korean brokerage Shinhan Securities wrote in a note last week that it expected "even stronger earnings" from Samsung next year.
"Samsung Electronics now appears all set for a rapid increase in market share," the Shinhan analysts said accordng to AFP . They pointed to the company's rising output of HBM4, its newest generation of high-bandwidth memory chips.
Samsung, domestic rival SK hynix and US-based Micron control the global market for HBM, which is used in AI data centres. "The transition to next-generation HBM will open up the opportunity to renegotiate prices" for Samsung, "helping to guarantee solid margins," the note said.
In August, Samsung unveiled a buyback of as much as $80bn of its shares. The company said the plan would ensure that the "fruits of the company's growth translate into tangible benefits" for shareholders.
Earlier this year, Samsung headed off a strike at home by agreeing to pay semiconductor staff bonuses equal to 10.5% of operating profit. About 78,000 of its 125,000 domestic employees qualify for a payout of roughly $370,000 each this year, based on market profit estimates.
In Taiwan, union representatives said hundreds of workers at a Micron plant had backed strike action after bonus negotiations with the US company broke down.

### 7. ★★★☆☆ Nobel peace laureate says US sanctions on ICC 'unacceptable'

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top / Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T15:23:47+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, The South African former ICC judge was awarded the prize on Friday
Navi Pillay, the winner of this year&#x27;s Nobel Peace Prize, has called US sanctions on the International Criminal Court (ICC) "unheard of" and "unacceptable".
The South African former ICC judge was awarded the prize on Friday "for her efforts to promote peace and international law", which paved the way for a "more comprehensive global legal order", the Nobel committee said.
US President Donald Trump expressed his disatisfaction with the pick and hours later the US unveiled sweeping sanctions in a significant acceleration of its attempt to "dismantle" what it described as a "rogue" institution.
The ICC said the measures were an assault on the rule of law and the foundations of the international legal order.
Pillay, speaking via video link during an event at the Nobel Peace Center in Oslo on Saturday, said: "There is so much tension and anxiety over the continued existence of international courts, mainly the International Criminal Court in The Hague.
"Most importantly, sanctions have been announced by one country against the judges, just for the judicial orders they issue. This is unheard of, unacceptable."
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio earlier said the sanctions - which include a ban on US entities making transactions with the ICC - were intended to "defend US sovereignty" and accused the court of seeking to prosecute Americans.
The US has already sanctioned 17 ICC judges and officials, but this move sanctions the court itself as an institution and is intended to inhibit its ability to operate financially.
However, the US has provided a six-month grace period before some of these latest sanctions kick in, meaning member states can keep paying for its operations - a move Washington says is to allow countries time to negotiate a "solution to address" US concerns.
The Trump administration claims that the ICC is run by what it calls globalists who threaten American sovereignty.
It is furious over a past investigation into actions of US soldiers in Afghanistan the court has since closed and by its arrest warrants for Israeli officials including Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu. The new sanctions, however, do not say that those warrants must be withdrawn before the measures can be lifted.
UN Secretary General Antonio Guterres called the US decision "a serious blow to our collective pursuit of international criminal justice, of which the ICC is a key pillar".
The UK joined Canada, Denmark, Germany, France, Italy, Japan and the Netherlands in saying they "regret and strongly disagree with" the sanctions.
Figure caption, Watch: Who is Nobel Peace Prize winner Navi Pillay?
Pillay, 85, also served as UN rights chief, taking part in high-profile work considering allegations of human rights violations, including in Rwanda, Myanmar and Gaza.
Her early work included defending anti-apartheid activists in South Africa, and on Friday she became the country&#x27;s fifth Peace Prize winner - following in the footsteps of Nelson Mandela and Archbishop Desmond Tutu.
"If you clap a little bit more I may share this prize with Trump," she joked after receiving the prize, a reference to last year&#x27;s winner, Venezuelan opposition figure María Corina Machado, deciding to gift her prize to the US president.
Trump called the announcement of Pillay as 2026&#x27;s winner "disgraceful and embarassing", and an "indelible stain on the country of Norway".
He has long indicated he thought he should have been awarded the prestigious prize, and was nominated by several allies for it last year.
US unveils sanctions on ICC in move court condemns as &#x27;assault on rule of law&#x27;
&#x27;Stain on the country&#x27;: Trump criticises Norway over Nobel Peace Prize

### 9. ★★★☆☆ Pentagon updates death toll of troops during Iran war to 21 - The Hill

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T21:59:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The Pentagon updated the American service member death toll of the U.S. military’s operations against Iran to 21 late Friday, raising the tally in the department’s public database.
The two noncombat deaths newly added to the Pentagon’s Defense Casualty Analysis System (DCAS) were Maj. Sorffly Davius and an unidentified Navy sailor, a U.S. official familiar with the matter, who spoke on condition of anonymity, told The Hill Saturday. Davius served in the 42nd Infantry Division at Camp Buehring, Kuwait and died of “undetermined causes” in March.
The Pentagon acknowledged Davius’s death earlier this year, at the time stating the major had been deployed to Kuwait last summer in support of Operation Spartan Shield. But the department did not immediately add him to the DCAS database, which has come under fire from Democrats in Congress .
The official did not identify the Navy sailor, but the service member was a woman between the ages of 25 and 30, according to DCAS.
Since the U.S. war with Iran kicked off in late February, 865 U.S. service members have been injured, according to DCAS.
Sgt. Devin Seibel — assigned to the Air Ambulance Company, 2nd Battalion, 4th Combat Aviation Brigade, Fort Carson, Colo. — died in late May at Erbil Air Base, Iraq, during a “training-related incident, the Pentagon said this year.
Seibel’s death was not included in the DCAS database earlier this year but has since been added into the portal under Operation Inherent Resolve.
Last month, The Washington Post reported that more U.S. service members had died in the Middle East during the war in Iran than what the Pentagon had disclosed on DCAS. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth slammed the news outlet and called the report “DISGUSTING and FAKE.”
Lawmakers have scrutinized the DCAS database and the way the Pentagon has reported casualties since the Iran war began on Feb. 28, particularly after the portal briefly excluded names of several U.S. service members who were killed by Iranian attacks. The Pentagon later restored their deaths to the portal.
This summer, the Pentagon divided the deaths and injuries in the war with Iran into two separate categories. One for Operation Epic Fury, the name of the Trump administration’s conflict with Tehran, and “Overseas Operations,” listing the casualties since July 7.

Emergency Oil Releases Risk Draining the World’s Last Supply Cushion
The IEA is releasing another 100 million barrels of emergency oil and fuels, but global inventories are rapidly shrinking amid the prolonged Hormuz crisis.
The U.S. Strategic Petroleum Reserve has fallen to 331.2 million barrels, its lowest level since 1983, approaching the estimated operational minimum of 250&ndash;300 million barrels.
Aramco warns the global oil supply cushion is dangerously thin, with inventories potentially taking two years to replenish once Hormuz reopens, leaving markets vulnerable to further shocks.
Earlier this week, the International Energy Agency, after consulting with G7, said it would release another 100 million barrels of diesel, gasoline, and crude oil. The release is part of a plan announced in March to release 400 million barrels of oil in response to the Hormuz squeeze. But there is a problem with those releases—storage is emptying.
When the U.S. administration began releasing oil from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve, a flurry of reports followed, citing experts who warned the level of oil in the SPR was already low enough for concern, and any further draws would be risky. As of the start of October, the amount of oil in the strategic U.S. reserve stood at 331.2 million barrels, which was the lowest since 1983, following the latest release of over 9 million barrels. The releases are part of a 172-million-barrel release the United States committed to earlier this year.
The problem is that 331.2 million barrels is quite close to what experts say is the operational minimum for the storage system—between 250 and 300 million barrels. Letting oil levels fall near the operational minimum is dangerous because this begins to affect the storage infrastructure, and not in a good way. To avoid that, inventories need to be replenished—and to be able to respond to any future supply crunches, of course. Related: U.S. Oil Drilling Continues to Inch Upward
The U.S. strategic oil reserve, however, is not the only one reaching minimum levels. Global inventories are on the decline, too. According to the IEA’s latest monthly oil report , from September, global oil inventories dropped by 95 million barrels in August, bringing the total draws since February to 507 million barrels, translating into 2.8 million barrels daily. The one piece of good news that the IEA had in that report was that OECD commercial inventories rose by 23 million barrels, offsetting a 19-million-barrel draw in strategic reserves.
However, the good news did not last long. Earlier this week, Aramco’s chief executive Amin Nasser warned that the world’s oil inventories were running dangerously low—and implied the prospect of refilling them was rather distant.
“The system is already straining,” Nasser told the Energy Intelligence Forum in London. “And, with precious little else the world can turn to, the supply resilience cushion is scarily thin.” The replenishment of drained inventories, Nasser noted, could take up to two years, and that only after the Strait of Hormuz reopens. While the world waits for that to happen, it is draining its inventories, exposing itself to much more severe supply shocks if the war drags on into 2027.
“IEA Member governments still have significant levels of publicly held emergency oil stocks – equivalent to around 1.1 billion barrels, including over 200 million barrels of diesel,” the agency’s head, Fatih Birol, said this week, following his meeting with agency members to discuss the new release of oil and fuel. “The IEA stands ready to release more of these stocks to the market if and when required.”
This was meant to reassure the market that there will be no shortage of, notably, diesel. However, it might well spark concern instead of allaying fears of shortages, because global inventories are also dangerously close to their operational minimum and that cannot be breached because once you breach it, you have no global inventory system.
The world has drawn over 1 billion barrels of crude from inventories since the start of the war between the United States and Israel, and Iran. Most of that came from commercial inventories, Aramco’s Nasser said this week. That leaves less than 6 billion barrels still in storage—and those 6 billion barrels are needed to stay where they are to keep the storage system functional. The world, it seems, has worn its oil supply cushion through.
No wonder, then, that a lot of countries, notably in Asia, are now in a race to build their oil inventories. Asia is especially vulnerable to adverse events in the Middle East because most of its oil imports come from the region. With that severely compromised, Asian economies, especially Southeast Asian ones, have been struggling to secure fuel for their economies and populations, and to keep them relatively affordable.
“The most advanced efforts are support for the development of oil stockpiling systems in each country and regional joint stockpiling on (a) voluntary basis,” nine governments from Southeast Asia said in a statement this week, as quoted by Reuters. In other words, they will be doing what the IEA is doing, to be able to better respond to future crises. But here is the thing—that oil that these countries will be stockpiling has to come from somewhere and not cost an arm and a leg. It would take a while before prices return to more palatable levels for Southeast Asian governments—or any other governments that would need to refill storage.

### 12. ★★★☆☆ Ship captains and crews crossing Hormuz earn so much they're 'almost being viewed as mercenaries' - Fortune

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Energy / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 2

Ship captains and crews transiting the Strait of Hormuz make so much danger pay that they’re ‘almost being viewed as mercenaries’
Iran&#x27;s medium-sized oil tankers continue to wait off the coast of Bandar Abbas at the Strait of Hormuz in Hormozgan Province, Iran on September 9, 2026. Fatemeh Bahrami/Anadolu via Getty Images
The high-risk, high-reward business of moving oil through the Strait of Hormuz has gotten riskier and more rewarding.
Despite steady attacks from Iran, oil tankers and other commercial vessels have continued flowing through the narrow waterway. With protection from the U.S. military and help from pipelines bypassing the strait, Persian Gulf oil flows returned to prewar levels .
But seeking to reassert its leverage over the global oil chokepoint, Iran has stepped up drone and missile strikes on ships recently, including those outside the immediate vicinity of Hormuz.
While traffic has dipped in response, ships continue to cross, with many making shuttle runs in and out of the Gulf to unload oil cargoes via ship-to-ship transfers. Then other vessels deliver the barrels to customers.
For those onboard the ships willing to tolerate the risks in the Gulf, a hefty payday awaits. Tanker captains can earn $100,000 a month for transiting the strait, plus a $50,000 bonus for each trip, sources told the Financial Times .
That “danger money” is up from regular pay of about $15,000 a month, and is necessary for shipowners to keep their fleets crewed.
Meanwhile, sailors typically make at little as $1,500 a month. But trips through Hormuz can bump up earnings by at least four to six times their normal rates, the report said.
Other hot spots also offer hazard enhancements, albeit less than what’s available for Hormuz trips. According to the FT , captains and sailors get double pay in the southern Red Sea , where Houthis have been attacking Saudi ships, and in the Gulf of Oman, where many ship-to-ship transfers take place after Hormuz shuttle runs.
Because many ships and their crews are dedicated to the shuttle runs, they are in constant peril—allowing them to stack up all the bonuses and danger money for each trip.
A source told the FT that those brave enough to stomach the near-constant threat of attacks are “almost being viewed as mercenaries,” though some unwilling crew members are reportedly being pressured to stay onboard.
Since the Iran war started on Feb. 28, at least 93 ships have been hit, and 24 sailors have been killed, according to the International Maritime Organization.
In addition to the hazard pay for crews, shipowners are also paying for insurance, and the war risk can command 6%-10% of a ship’s value—translating to up to $20 million for a supertanker sailing through the Gulf.
All the added costs have made shipping expensive too. Freight rates for cargoes crossing the Strait of Hormuz hit a record high of $1.3 million per day, up from last year’s daily rate of $20,000-$50,000.
All the ships needed to get oil out of the Gulf have also contributed to a global tanker shortage that’s spiked freight rates around the world.
Shipbroker Gibson  pointed out that hiring a tanker to travel from the U.S. to China costs about $80 million, more than the $74 million price tag for a standard SpaceX Falcon 9 launch. Brokerage SSY estimated that rates are now the highest since the advent of the supertanker in the 1960s, even after adjusting for inflation.
Shipping costs have become so extreme that oil producers and even commodities traders are looking to own their own tankers to control the expenses.
But if freight costs get too high, they could erase the margins that can be earned from oil and refined fuels. For instance, European refiner Repsol saw its margin drop from $36 per barrel in the third quarter to $15 in October, according to analysts at RBC. Further reduction in margins could eventually force refiners to reduce how much crude they process.
The hazard premium could get worse in the Persian Gulf. Analysts have warned the Iranian regime, facing an existential threat to its rule, could re-escalate the war and take back its grip on the oil trade.
Esfandyar Batmanghelidj, founder and CEO of the Bourse & Bazaar Foundation think tank, pointed out recently that Iran can still destroy regional oil infrastructure, such as drilling and refining capacity, and can escalate if President Donald Trump rejects diplomatic off-ramps.
“Iran did not wage a scorched earth campaign—but it may yet do so if the current situation persists for too long,”  he posted on X  late last month. “That is what everyone who actually follows this region and its dynamics is worried about.”
Fortune Daily breaks the traditional barrier between audience and newsroom. The show transforms Fortune ’s trusted reporting into actionable, conversational, and entertaining insights for an emerging class of business leaders. Watch here.
Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune , where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing.

### 13. ★★★☆☆ Supreme Court climate case could bankrupt oil companies, send gas prices soaring, experts warn - Fox News

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T20:05:53+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Energy group warns Suncor v. Boulder climate case could drive up gas prices nationwide
American Energy Institute CEO Jason Isaac warns the Supreme Court climate fight could open the door to lawsuits against energy companies, gas stations and other businesses, potentially raising costs for consumers.
A Supreme Court showdown over climate change lawsuits could leave oil companies bankrupt, gas stations facing financial ruin and Americans paying more at the pump if the justices allow cities and states to pursue billions in damages against the fossil fuel industry, energy policy experts warn.
"You would see mass exodus and that would create more scarcity with fuel, more so than we're seeing already today, higher prices," Jason Isaac, CEO of American Energy Institute, told Fox News Digital. "And that's really what this is about. It's about controlling these companies and stopping the use of hydrocarbons."
The Supreme Court heard arguments Monday in Suncor v. Boulder, a dispute over whether federal law prevents cities and states from suing oil companies under state law for alleged climate damage linked to emissions that cross state borders.
During oral arguments, Justice Clarence Thomas pressed Boulder's attorney, Kevin Russell, on whether the legal theory could expose businesses beyond oil producers to similar lawsuits, including large retailers.
WAVE OF OVERLOOKED 'LEFT-WING' LAWSUITS COULD COME WITH AN UNEXPECTED PRICE TAG FOR AMERICANS
Climate activists protester gather outside of the Supreme Court for oral arguments for Suncor v. Boulder on Oct. 5, 2026 (Elaine Mallon Fox News)
"Nothing in our theory prevents that," Russell acknowledged, although he noted that state tort law could impose additional limitations.
Justice Brett Kavanaugh separately raised concerns about the potential financial consequences of widespread litigation, warning that enough lawsuits could "bankrupt" defendants and questioning whether virtually any manufacturer or business could face similar claims.
The city and county of Boulder, Colo., sued oil giants ExxonMobil and Suncor Energy in 2018, accusing the companies of knowingly contributing to climate change while misleading the public about the alleged dangers of fossil fuels. The municipalities are seeking damages to help cover the mounting costs of climate-related harms . There are roughly 30 similar lawsuits pending in jurisdictions across the country, including cases in Portland and Baltimore.
Boulder's lawsuit alleged ExxonMobil and Suncor knew for decades about the climate risks associated with fossil fuels but misled the public about those risks. The complaint pointed to a 1977 internal memo from ExxonMobil that circulated among the company's highest-level managers. The memo reported that "current scientific opinion overwhelmingly favors" that fossil fuels contribute to the rise in CO2 emissions.
David Bookbinder, who previously served as counsel of record for Boulder but is no longer involved in the case, described the lawsuit as a way to implement an "indirect carbon tax" during a Federalist Society forum last year.
But Boulder has maintained that the case is not an attempt to regulate national climate policy, arguing instead that Colorado has the authority to hold companies accountable under state law for alleged harms suffered within its borders.
"Since the founding, states have had the power to provide tort remedies for injuries occurring within their borders even when the conduct causing those injuries occurred elsewhere," Russell told the justices.
Chief Justice John Roberts and the Supreme Court return to the bench as disputes over President Trump’s agenda remain a major focus. (Photo by Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)
CATHOLIC SCHOOLS, BANNED RIFLES AND ‘CLIMATE LAWFARE’: BLOCKBUSTER FIGHTS AWAIT SUPREME COURT
O.H. Skinner, executive director of the Alliance for Consumers, argued that the lawsuits are an attempt to accomplish through the courts what climate advocates have been unable to achieve through Congress.
"When you really boil it down, and you separate it from all the legal arguments, the advocates who push these cases are very clear," Skinner told Fox News Digital. "That it's an effort to get a backdoor carbon tax, because carbon taxes have never passed in Congress, or to bankrupt the energy industry."
Justice Samuel Alito has recused himself from the case without providing an explanation.
Isaac warned that a Supreme Court ruling in Boulder's favor could open "Pandora's box," allowing thousands of jurisdictions to pursue similar lawsuits. He said a 4-4 split could have a similar practical effect by leaving the lower court's ruling in place, although a tie would not establish nationwide precedent.
"There are over 90,000 levels of government — government entities just in the United States alone — that could also begin lawsuits against energy companies," Isaac told Fox News Digital. "Driving up cost to consumers because the cost to defend those would be astronomical."
Members of the Supreme Court pose for a group photo at the Supreme Court on Oct. 7, 2022. Seated from left: Associate Justice Sonia Sotomayor, Associate Justice Clarence Thomas, Chief Justice of the United States John G. Roberts Jr., Associate Justice Samuel A. Alito Jr. and Associate Justice Elena Kagan. Standing behind from left: Associate Justice Amy Coney Barrett, Associate Justice Neil M. Gorsuch, Associate Justice Brett M. Kavanaugh and Associate Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson. (USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect)
LAWSUITS COST EVERY AMERICAN HOUSEHOLD THOUSANDS OF DOLLARS. SUPREME COURT CAN HELP
Skinner argued that who could be sued could extend beyond oil producers to businesses across the energy supply chain, including gas stations, automakers and utilities that sell or use fossil fuels.
"From Boulder's perspective, anybody who's contributed to climate change would reliable, any sort of company, big or small, and the problem here is that to the left, climate change is everything and everything is climate change," Skinner told Fox News Digital. "So it's very hard to find a line for who isn't contributing to climate change."
But Isaac said the case is different from lawsuits against tobacco or opioid companies because greenhouse gas emissions come from countless sources around the world and cross state and national borders, making it difficult to isolate responsibility for climate-related damage.
"Emissions are a global phenomenon," Isaac told Fox News Digital.
CONSERVATIVE GROUPS SAY JUSTICE KAGAN CANNOT BE IMPARTIAL IN UPCOMING SUPREME COURT CLIMATE LITIGATION
ExxonMobil and Suncor argue that because greenhouse gas emissions travel globally, Colorado cannot use state law to hold companies liable for emissions originating outside its borders. The companies contend that such disputes should instead be governed by federal law.
Meanwhile, other states such as Utah have barred these types of state tort lawsuits from being pursued.
"If the energy companies were to lose and Colorado were to win, this would in effect drive up the prices of gas all across the country," Utah Attorney General Derek Brown told Fox News Digital. "And so those kind of decisions ultimately, it's the province of Congress."
Elaine Mallon is a writer for Fox News Digital and Fox Business covering national politics.

### 14. ★★★☆☆ What has Trump’s Board of Peace achieved in a year since Gaza ‘ceasefire’? - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Top / NPR World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T23:03:43+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share What has Trump’s Board of Peace achieved in a year since Gaza ‘ceasefire’? on social media
A tent camp shelters displaced Palestinians in al-Mawasi on the coast of the southern Gaza Strip on October 7, 2026 [Ramadan Abed/Reuters]
It has been one year since United States President Donald Trump’s Board of Peace (BoP) brokered a “ceasefire” aimed at eventually ending Israel’s genocidal war in Gaza.
However, rather than receiving relief and a chance to rebuild, Palestinians in the enclave continue to face attacks and be uprooted as Israeli forces have expanded control within the territory.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 Global protests mark October 7 attack with calls for Gaza ceasefire
list 2 of 4 ‘War lives in me’: Gaza playwright wins prestigious award in London
list 3 of 4 Israeli settlers take Palestinian olives, and their harvest traditions
list 4 of 4 Israel kills or wounds Palestinian every 90 minutes in Gaza since ceasefire
Trump first proposed the BoP in September 2025, saying it would stabilise Gaza and ensure lasting peace.
In November, a United Nations Security Council resolution recognised the board as a “transitional administration” in Gaza.
But what has the board achieved? Here’s what we know:
People search the rubble of a collapsed building following overnight Israeli airstrikes in Gaza City on October 9, 2026, before which the Israeli military had warned residents to evacuate the area (AFP) What were the BoP’s goals?
The BoP’s mission was laid out in Trump’s 20-point Gaza peace plan , as was the “ceasefire” between Israel and Hamas. The ceasefire began on October 10, 2025.
The plan envisioned steps for each side to take that could eventually create a “credible pathway” to Palestinian statehood.
It required Israeli forces to withdraw to about 50 percent of the Gaza Strip and cease aerial and artillery bombardments. It also required Israel to release 250 Palestinian prisoners and another 1,700 people jailed from Gaza since October 7, 2023, the day that Hamas led attacks into southern Israel and Israel began the war. The plan proposed reopening border crossings to allow much-needed aid into Gaza.
The peace plan required Hamas to release all Israeli captives. It also proposed a later phase in which the group would disarm and its members would be granted amnesty or a safe exit from Gaza.
The peace plan established the BoP as a transitional governing body until the Palestinian Authority, which governs parts of the occupied West Bank, could take over.
An International Stabilization Force (ISF), led by the US, was supposed to take over security in Gaza from withdrawing Israeli forces, the plan stated. It also proposed the reconstruction and redevelopment of Gaza, whose people, it said, “have suffered enough”.
In February, the BoP’s members, who each joined in exchange for a $1bn annual fee, met in Washington, DC, for its first summit, at which member states pledged $7bn in funding although aid agencies said reconstructing Gaza would cost closer to $70bn.
On the sidelines of the United Nations General Assembly in New York last month, the BoP also unveiled a 66-project recovery plan worth $2.4bn.
The BoP’s achievements are tied to the success of the Gaza peace plan.
Under that, Hamas has released all 168 living captives and Israel has freed Palestinian prisoners.
Hamas has also announced the dissolution of its government in the Gaza Strip although Israel has dismissed that as a “stunt”.
Other goals under the peace plan have been only partially met.
Governance has been hampered. While a National Committee for the Administration of Gaza (NCAG), meant to act as the governing authority in Gaza, was founded, Israel has not granted it entry into the Strip. The NCAG reports directly to the board and is led by Chief Commissioner Ali Shaath and other Palestinian technocrats. It operates from Cairo. Israel has not stated why the committee is not permitted to enter Gaza.
Aid remains restricted. About 227 aid trucks enter the Strip daily when 600 trucks were supposed to, according to the Integrated Food Security Phase Classification (IPC) hunger monitor.
Israel has classified thousands of basic items, such as tents and essential equipment like ventilators, as “dual use” items, which means they have military and civilian applications, and has restricted their flow into the Strip. About 1.4 million Palestinians – or most of Gaza’s more than 2 million people – will suffer hunger until at least December, the IPC has noted. That is 200,000 more than those who faced hunger in May, suggesting a significantly deteriorating situation.
Observers argued that the BoP has entirely failed to secure the “ceasefire” as Israeli attacks have continued almost daily on the Strip.
Since October 10, 2025, more than 1,400 Palestinians, mostly women and children, have been killed. In the three years since the war began, more than 74,000 Palestinians have been killed, and thousands more are missing and presumed dead under the rubble.
Israel says its continuing attacks target Hamas members, but the group vehemently denies the claims.
Israel also now occupies about 60 percent of the Strip, more than the 50 percent agreed in Trump’s peace plan. The “Yellow Line”, which demarcates the area of Gaza under Israeli occupation, has been pushed farther into the Strip, causing further displacement and squeezing Gaza’s population into an ever-shrinking area. Palestinians unknowingly crossing the “Yellow Line” have been shot dead.
In December, Israeli Defence Minister Israel Katz said the army “will never fully leave” the Strip.
Mohamad Elmasry at the Doha Institute of Graduate Studies told Al Jazeera that Israel is simply continuing a broader policy of “ethnic cleansing” in Gaza and the occupied West Bank. “They are making life miserable,” he said, adding: “This is all by design.”
Consequently, the BoP has also failed to ensure Hamas disarms so phase two of the peace plan could begin.
Although Hamas has agreed to hand its weapons over to the NCAG, it rejects the “disarmament” clause because Israel has not ceased its attacks or withdrawn from the Strip as agreed. At the same time, Israel has said it will not move forward with the plan if Hamas does not disarm, leaving the two sides in a standoff.
Meanwhile, no stabilisation force has been deployed. Six countries – Indonesia, Morocco, Kazakhstan, Albania, Kosovo and Uganda – have so far committed personnel to form a security force of 20,000 soldiers and 12,000 police headed by the US military’s Central Command (CENTCOM).
There has been no meaningful reconstruction in Gaza. Palestinians continue to live in tent camps infested with rodents and insects with little to shield them from intense heat, or rain.
The Trump administration and BoP have echoed Israel’s rejection of the United Nations agency for Palestinian refugees, or UNRWA, which has long provided lifesaving humanitarian aid, education, development programmes and social services in Gaza and employs about 11,000 Palestinians.
The BoP said last month that it will not work with UNRWA, essentially sidelining the agency.
A BoP official told The Associated Press news agency that UNRWA could continue working in Gaza if its programmes were deemed relevant but the agency’s “days are over”.
In June, the UN General Assembly voted overwhelmingly to extend UNRWA’s mandate until 2029. The BoP itself is mandated to oversee the peace process until December 2027 when its progress will be reviewed for possible renewal by the UN.
Analysts and rights groups have raised concerns that the BoP could take over all aspects of Gaza’s rebuilding. Countries too, including France and other European nations, have called out any attempts to replace the UN in the Strip.
But Elmasry said no country is willing to take action. “There are a lot of international actors that do not want to cross the US,” he said. That essentially leaves the fate of more than 2 million Palestinians to Trump, he added.
Ultimately, the BoP has “failed”, said Simon Mabon, a professor at Lancaster University.
“The failure to implement its own plan to end the Gaza conflict demonstrates its impotence and the inability to ensure that Israel and, to a lesser extent, Hamas, remain bound by their own commitments [and] has allowed the genocide to continue,” he concluded.

### 16. ★★★☆☆ 12 killed and over 300 hurt in latest attack on Riyadh airport, Saudi authorities say - AP News

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: AP World / GN Top / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T05:05:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Attack on Riyadh airport killed 12 people and wounded over 300 more, Saudi authorities say Saudi authorities say the latest attack on the international airport in the capital, Riyadh, has killed 12 people and injured over 300. Their statement says the dead on Saturday included four Saudi citizens as well as two Bangladeshi nationals, a Palestinian, a U.S. citizen, an Egyptian, a Jordanian, a Syrian and a Sudanese. It said some of those injured were in critical condition. The attack — the latest of several in the past week on King Khalid International Airport — is expected to increase Saudi pressure on allies including the United States to provide military support for the kingdom.
Attack on Riyadh airport killed 12 people and wounded over 300 more, Saudi authorities say
This image from video obtained and verified by AP shows thick black smoke and flames rising from a fire at King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, Friday, Oct. 9, 2026. (UGC via AP)
CAIRO (AP) — The latest attack on the international airport in Saudi Arabia's capital of Riyadh killed 12 people and injured over 300 others, civil aviation authorities said early Sunday, with some in critical condition.
The attack was the deadliest in any Gulf Arab country since the start of the Iran war, whose fronts have expanded to include the recent surge in fighting between the Iran-backed Houthi rebels in Yemen and Saudi-backed government forces there.
The statement, which did not assign blame for the attack, said the dead included four Saudi citizens as well as two Bangladeshi nationals, a Palestinian, a U.S. citizen, an Egyptian, a Jordanian, a Syrian and a Sudanese. It said the injured were being treated at several hospitals.
The attack — the latest of several in the past week on King Khalid International Airport that killed four people — is expected to increase Saudi pressure on allies including the United States to provide military support for the kingdom.
Shortly after the attack on Saturday afternoon, President Donald Trump said he was evaluating whether the U.S. should join Saudi strikes against the Houthis. "We may. We're going to look at it," Trump said. "We just found out about the recent attack. So we'll make a decision. We move very quickly."
A missile struck an airport complex housing terminals 3 and 4 for domestic flights, a regional official said, speaking on condition of anonymity because they weren't authorized to speak to the media.
The surge in attacks, including against other Saudi airports, military facilities and oil infrastructure, has come in the week since a Saudi-led coalition launched a major military offensive against the Houthis. There was no immediate statement from the rebels, who earlier Saturday accused Saudi Arabia of striking Sanaa International Airport in the Houthi-held Yemeni capital. The rebels at times take days to claim responsibility.
Saudi civil aviation authorities earlier said airport operations were suspended. An official notice to aviators said the suspension was through midnight. The airport had a heavy police presence, with all entrances blocked, and lights around the complex were dimmed.
Flights diverted elsewhere, the Flightradar24 tracker said. Countries including the United States, Britain, Germany, Australia and Canada warned citizens to avoid the airport. Statements later turned to condolences.
Later on Saturday, the Saudi-led coalition said a projectile had fallen near King Fahd International Airport in Dammam and a Houthi-launched ballistic missile had been intercepted in Al-Kharj governorate, southeast of Riyadh.
The attacks on Saudi Arabia, the world's top oil exporter, have rattled markets and the region. And yet the Saudi energy ministry hours after the latest one was still promoting a major international energy conference set to begin in Riyadh on Sunday.
Fighting between the Houthis and Saudi-backed Yemeni government forces has escalated, with the Saudi-led coalition providing air support for government forces attempting to reverse recent Houthi gains along Yemen's Red Sea coast. Those gains gave the rebels better surveillance of shipping on a corridor that is a crucial alternative to the Strait of Hormuz, where Iran has been attacking ships.
Though the Houthis have told the AP and others they are only targeting Saudi shipping as part of a blockade in retaliation for one imposed on them, concerns about wider attacks persist. The Houthis previously attacked other ships after the war in Gaza began, calling it solidarity with Palestinians. A U.S. bombing campaign sharply limited that.
Saudi Arabia has run low on interceptors, officials say
Saudi Arabia is running low on missile interceptors and has asked France, Britain, Pakistan and Egypt to deploy air defense support to the region, regional officials told the AP last month, with one noting that the U.S. has seen its own stock dwindle during the war with Iran.
Officials have said U.S. assistance has included intelligence sharing, target identification and planning for the latest offensive against the Houthis.
Earlier Saturday, Saudi Arabia said its foreign minister and Omani counterpart spoke by phone about the importance of "supporting diplomatic solutions to de-escalate tensions" in the region. Oman, which lies across the Strait of Hormuz from Iran, has played a mediator role at times in the region.
The Houthis this week warned global airlines "for the last time" about the potential consequences of operating in Saudi airspace, which they described as a theater for their military operations.
Before the latest attack, Air India, FlyDubai, Lufthansa Group and Pakistan International Airlines were among airlines suspending flights to and from Riyadh and other Saudi airports for varying periods.
The European Union Aviation Safety Agency had expanded its guidance on Saudi airspace, noting the Houthis' ability to strike targets deeper inside the kingdom, including Riyadh, and a "greater focus on aviation infrastructure."
The missile and drone attacks and interception efforts "create an increased risk of misidentification of civil aircraft," it said.
Two regional officials said Houthi attacks on the Riyadh airport on Thursday damaged three planes. The officials spoke on condition of anonymity because they weren't authorized to brief the media. The Saudi national carrier said one of its aircraft was damaged while it was on the ground, with no passengers on board.
Meanwhile, Saudi authorities warned the public this week that "filming, publishing or sharing information related to countering missiles and drones and their impact sites exposes you to legal liability."
Anna reported from Jerusalem. Associated Press writers Victoria Eastwood in Cairo, Collin Binkley in Washington and Sally Abou AlJoud in Beirut contributed to this report.

### 18. ★★★☆☆ Mystery surrounds young worker’s death at Russia lab studying severe diseases - AP News

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: AP World / GN Geopolitics / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T20:13:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Russian Offensive Campaign Assessment, October 10, 2026
Russian President Vladimir Putin used his phone call with US President Donald Trump on October 9 to falsely blame Ukraine for the lack of peace negotiations’ progress despite Russia’s continued unwillingness to engage in meaningful negotiations or make any concessions to end the war in Ukraine. Russian Presidential Aide Yuriy Ushakov claimed that Putin told Trump that Ukraine’s alleged long-range strikes against Russian election infrastructure during the September 2026 Russian State Duma elections “thwarted the possibility” of “immediately” resuming peace negotiations — attempting to malign Ukraine as the party responsible for halting peace negotiations.[1] There is, in fact, no evidence that Ukraine targeted Russian election infrastructure during the September 2026 Duma elections, and Russian claims that Ukraine attempted to target election infrastructure remain unfounded. Putin emphasized that Russia’s commitment to achieve all its war goals and objectives — which are tantamount to full Ukrainian and Western surrender — remain unchanged. Ukraine, the United States, and Europe have repeatedly presented various ceasefires and peace proposals with concessions to Russia — all of which the Kremlin has continuously rejected.[2] Moscow has insisted on painting Ukraine as the intransigent party in peace efforts despite the fact that Russia has not offered any concessions and has rejected numerous compromise proposals that the US and Ukraine have offered. Putin’s October 9 reiteration of his commitment to Russia’s unchanged war aims demonstrates the Kremlin’s continued unwillingness to accept any compromised peace proposals on any terms that fall short of fulfilling Russia’s maximalist demands of Ukraine’s complete capitulation.[3]
The Russian Ministry of Defense (MoD) is presenting a tailored weekly summary of the Russian forces’ exaggerated military successes aimed specifically at Russia’s domestic audience through Max – the new social media platform that the Russian state has developed to control the Russian internet. The Russian MoD published a rollup of Russian claimed battlefield successes on October 9 only on its Max channel, a Russian messenger platform which requires Russian or Belarusian phone numbers as a prerequisite for registration, and claimed that Russian forces seized two settlements in Ukraine between October 3 and 9 and another three over the past 24 hours.[4] The Russian MoD also claimed that Russian forces thwarted Ukrainian forces' attempts to conduct rotations or break out of the claimed encirclements in Dobropillya, Donetsk Oblast, and near Chorne (northeast of Kharkiv City).[5] The Russian MoD claimed that the Russian Northern Grouping of Forces (GoF) seized 414 square kilometers and 20 settlements since encircling Ukrainian forces in Kharkiv Oblast — an exaggerated claim that has no basis in reality, as Ukrainian forces and Russian milbloggers themselves have refuted or questioned claims that Russian forces encircled any Ukrainian forces in northern Kharkiv Oblast.[6] The MoD rollup also highlighted the Russian long-range strikes against defense industry facilities, telecommunications centers, and dry cargo ships in Ukraine over the past week.[7] The Russian MoD notably only published this weekly summary on its Max channel rather than on its internationally-facing Telegram channel, indicating that the Russian MoD likely aims to highlight and exaggerate the Russian forces’ weekly military achievements specifically for the Russian domestic audience and present Russian continuing battlefield successes despite evidence that Russian advances have significantly slowed.[8] The apparent difference in messaging suggests that the Kremlin seeks to minimize the amount of international scrutiny it receives from its baseless claims of Russian successes, though the MoD still makes highly exaggerated claims on its Telegram channel — claims that ISW regularly refutes.[9]
US President Donald Trump’s October 9 announcement about the diesel export deal between the United States and Russia disrupted the negotiations meeting between a Ukrainian delegation and US Special Envoy to the Middle East Steve Witkoff and former Senior Advisor to the US President Jared Kushner in Miami. Ukraine’s negotiating team met with Witkoff, Kushner, and the representatives of the E3 (France, Germany, and the United Kingdom), the European Commission, and NATO in Miami, Florida, on October 9 to discuss Ukraine’s preparations ahead of Winter 2026-2027, peace proposals and proposals for “territorial solutions,” security guarantees, Ukraine’s post-war reconstruction, and long-term Ukraine’s and Europe’s security agreements.[10] Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty journalist Alex Raufoglou reported, citing unspecified sources, that there will be no second day of negotiations and that the talks between Ukraine’s delegation and the United States ended sooner than planned.[11] Three people familiar with the matter told Financial Times that Trump’s announcement about a diesel export deal with Russia ”blindsided” the Ukrainian delegation.[12] Ukrainian Presidential Communications Adviser Dmytro Lytvyn confirmed on October 10 that Ukraine’s negotiating team is heading back to Ukraine.[13]
Russian President Vladimir Putin used his phone call with US President Donald Trump on October 9 to falsely blame Ukraine for the lack of peace negotiations’ progress despite Russia’s continued unwillingness to engage in meaningful negotiations or make any concessions to end the war in Ukraine.
The Russian Ministry of Defense (MoD) is presenting a tailored weekly summary of the Russian forces’ exaggerated military successes aimed specifically at Russia’s domestic audience through Max – the new social media platform that the Russian state has developed to control the Russian internet.
US President Donald Trump’s October 9 announcement about the diesel export deal between the United States and Russia disrupted the negotiations meeting between a Ukrainian delegation and US Special Envoy to the Middle East Steve Witkoff and former Senior Advisor to the US President Jared Kushner in Miami.
Ukrainian forces conducted drone strikes against oil and railway infrastructure in Rostov Oblast on October 10. Russian forces launched 160 drones against Ukraine overnight.
Ukrainian forces recently advanced in the Borova direction.
We do not report in detail on Russian war crimes because these activities are well-covered in Western media and do not directly affect the military operations we are assessing and forecasting. We will continue to evaluate and report on the effects of these criminal activities on the Ukrainian military and the Ukrainian population and specifically on combat in Ukrainian urban areas. We utterly condemn Russian violations of the laws of armed conflict and the Geneva Conventions and crimes against humanity even though we do not describe them in these reports.
Ukrainian forces conducted drone strikes against oil infrastructure in Rostov Oblast on October 10. Geolocated footage published on October 10 shows a fire at the NZNP oil-loading terminal in Rostov-on-Don (roughly 210 kilometers from the frontline) following a reported Ukrainian drone strike.[14]
Russian officials claimed that Ukrainian forces struck railway infrastructure in Rostov and Bryansk oblasts on October 10. Rostov Oblast Governor Yuri Slyusar claimed on October 10 that Ukrainian drone strikes damaged a railway bridge in Rostov-on-Don and two vessels in Azov (20 kilometers from Rostov-on-Don). [15] The local branch of Russian state-owned Russian Railways confirmed that Ukrainian drone strikes damaged a railway bridge in Rostov-on-Don, suspending rail traffic.[16] Bryansk Oblast Governor Yegor Kovalchuk claimed on October 10 that Ukrainian forces struck a railway bridge in Vygonichsky Raion (roughly 80 kilometers from the international border), disrupting rail traffic.[17]
Drone debris from an October 9 strike reportedly caused a fire and shut down an automobile factory in Kaluga Oblast. The Russian automotive holding company AGR Automotive Group reported on October 9 that it is suspending the production of Tenet automobiles at its auto plant in Kaluga City(roughly 290 kilometers from the frontline) for safety reasons after drone debris reportedly fell on the plant during Russian air defense operations.[18] AGR's Kaluga auto plant is located in the Grabtsevo Industrial Park in Kaluga City alongside the Yandex Kaluga data center that Ukrainian forces struck on October 9.[19]
Russian objective: Create defensible buffer zones in Sumy Oblast along the international border
A Russian milblogger denied Russian claims that Russian forces control forested areas near Kyianytsia (north of Sumy City). [20] Ukrainian military officials previously refuted claims from the Russian Ministry of Defense (MoD) that Russian forces seized Kyiantysia on October 9.[21]
Ukrainian forces continued their intermediate-range strike campaign against Russian air defenses in Bryansk Oblast. The Ukrainian General Staff reported that Ukrainian forces struck an electronic warfare system near Trukhanovo (roughly 47 kilometers from the international border) on October 9 or the night of October 9 to 10.[22]
Russian objective: Push Ukrainian forces back from the international border to create a defensible buffer zone with Belgorod Oblast and approach Kharkiv City
Russian forces recently conducted infiltration missions in northern Kharkiv Oblast. Ukrainian forces struck Russian servicemembers south of Volokhivka (northeast of Kharkiv City), according to geolocated footage published on October 10, after what ISW assesses was a Russian infiltration mission.[23] Ukrainian forces also struck a Russian position east of Komisarove (northeast of Velykyi Burluk), according to geolocated footage published on October 9, after what ISW assesses was an infiltration mission.[24]
The Russian Ministry of Defense (MoD) and Russian milbloggers continue to make baseless claims about the alleged large-scale encirclement between the Vovchansk and Velykyi Burluk directions. The Russian MoD claimed on October 10 that the Russian Northern Grouping of Forces (GoF) seized Lukashove (north of Velykyi Burluk) and Lobanivka (south of Lobanivka).[25] Russian sources credited elements of the 344th Motorized Rifle Regiment (69th Motorized Rifle Division, 6th Combined Arms Army [CAA], Leningrad Military District [LMD]) and elements of the 34th Separate Motorized Rifle Brigade (49th CAA, Southern Military District [SMD], under the operational control of the Northern GoF) with the claimed seizures of Lukashove and Lobanivka, respectively.[26] A Russian milblogger claimed that the alleged seizures of Lobanivka and Lukashove will allow Russian forces to outflank Riznykove (between Lobanivka and Lukashove) from the north and south.[27] The milblogger claimed that there are only five settlements in the alleged encirclement in northern Kharkiv Oblast that Russian forces have not yet seized. A Kremlin-affiliated Russian milblogger stated that the claimed seizures of Lobanivka and Lukashove require further confirmation, however.[28] The Russian MoD and milbloggers have regularly made baseless claims of a large-scale encirclement in the Velykyi Burluk and Vovchansk directions, despite no evidence to support their claims.[29]
Russian objective: Cross the Oskil River in Kharkiv Oblast and push westward into eastern Kharkiv Oblast and northern Donetsk Oblas t
Russian forces recently conducted infiltration missions in the Kupyansk direction. Ukrainian forces struck a Russian-occupied building in Tyshchenkivka (northwest of Kupyansk), according to geolocated footage posted on October 9.[30] Ukrainian forces also struck a Russian-occupied building along the Pivdenna Railway on the northern outskirts of Kupyansk-Vuzlovyi (south of Kupyansk), according to geolocated footage published on October 9.[31] ISW assesses that both strikes occurred after Russian infiltration missions. Some Russian milbloggers continued to claim that Russian forces seized Kupyansk-Vuzlovyi, but some Russian sources claimed that fighting continues in the settlement and it is premature to talk about full Russian control over it.[32]
Ukrainian forces recently advanced in the Borova direction. Geolocated footage published on October 9 shows Russian forces striking a Ukrainian position northwest of Borova Andriivka (northeast of Borova), indicating that Ukrainian forces recently advanced in the area.[33]
Russian Main Effort: Capture Ukraine's Fortress Belt and complete the seizure of Donetsk Oblast
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #1 – Encircle the Fortress Belt from the northwest
Russian objective: Seize Lyman and cross the Siverskyi Donets River to support a wide encirclement of the Fortress Belt – assessed objective of the Russian 25th Combined Arms Army and Western Grouping of Forces elements
A prominent Russian milblogger assessed that Russian forces maintaining control over settlements along the Zherebets River, where Ukrainian forces are counterattacking is vital for Russian logistics and future efforts to seize Lyman. Several Russian milbloggers noted that Ukrainian forces are attacking near Zarichne (northeast of Lyman) and Yampil (southeast of Lyman).[34] A Kremlin-affiliated Russian milblogger assessed that the future Ukrainian liberation of Zarichne would fix Russian forces in the Svyati Hory National Nature Park, a large forested area that runs between Zarichne and Yampil and eastward into the Russian near rear, but noted that Russian forces in the forest are still able to receive supplies from Torske (just west of Zarichne on the other side of the Zherebets River) and Yampil.[35] The milblogger claimed that elements of the both the Russian Western and Southern groupings of forces, operating in the Lyman and Slovyansk directions, respectively, are striking Ukrainian forces attacking near Yampil. The milblogger assessed that Russia's loss of Zarichne and Yampil will make a new offensive against Lyman "impossible."
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #2 – Advance on the Fortress Belt from the east
Russian objective: Advance on Slovyansk, Kramatorsk, and Druzhkivka from the east – assessed objective of the Russian 3rd Combined Arms Army and Southern Grouping of Forces elements
A Kremlin-affiliated Russian milblogger acknowledged that Russian forces are trying to recapture Rai-Oleksandrivka, indicating that Ukrainian forces liberated the settlement.[36] The milblogger connected the Ukrainian liberation of Rai-Oleksandrivka to Ukrainian successes in the Lyman area with Operation Vivaldi. Ukrainian forces also continued counterattacks east of Kramatorsk near Yurkivka, Minkivka, and Vasyutynske, according to Russian milbloggers.[37]
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #3 – Advance on the Fortress Belt from the south
Russian objective: Advance on Kostyantynivka and Druzhkivka from the south and southwest, east of the Kazenyi Torets River – assessed objective of the Russian 8th Combined Arms Army and Southern Grouping of Forces elements
Russian forces continued offensive operations in the Kostyantynivka direction on October 9 and 10 but did not advance. [38]
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #4 – Encircle the Fortress Belt from the southwest
Russian objective: Conduct a wide encirclement of the Fortress Belt from near Dobropillya and Pokrovsk west of the Kazenyi Torets River – assessed objective of the Russian 51st Combined Arms Army and Central Grouping of Forces elements
Russian forces recently conducted an infiltration mission northeast of Dobropillya. Ukrainian forces struck a Russian position northeast of Dobropillya, according to geolocated footage published on October 10, after what ISW assesses was a Russian infiltration mission.[39] A Russian milblogger claimed on October 9 that Russian forces are consolidating positions near Vesele Pole (north of Dobropillya) &#8212; in line with the Russian MoD’s baseless allegations that Russian forces recently made significant advances to encircle the town.[40]
Russian Supporting Effort: Advance into Dnipropetrovsk Oblast
Russian objective: Interdict Ukrainian ground lines of communication that support Ukrainian positions in Donetsk Oblast and envelop Ukrainian positions in southwestern Donetsk Oblast – assessed objective of elements of the Russian Central and Eastern groupings of forces
Russian forces continued limited offensive operations in the Novopavlivka and Oleksandrivka directions on October 9 and 10 but did not advance.[41]
Russian objective: Maintain frontline positions, secure rear areas against Ukrainian strikes, and advance within tube artillery range of Zaporizhzhia City – assessed objective of the Russian Eastern and Dnepr Grouping of Forces
Russian milbloggers are issuing conflicting reports about the extent of Russian advances in the Hulyaipole direction. A Kremlin-affiliated Russian milblogger claimed on October 10 that Russian forces are advancing near Barvinivka and Voskresenka, two nearby settlements connected by the T-0408 highway that are northwest of Hulyaipole and 20 and 22 kilometers from ISW-assessed Russian advances, respectively.[42] Another Russian milblogger claimed on October 9, however, that Russian forces recently established fire control over a portion of the T-0408 road connecting Voskresenka and Tersyanka (4.4 kilometers north of Voskresenka), suggesting that Russian positions are further back from Voskresenka and Barvinivka than the Kremlin-affiliated milblogger claimed.[43]
Russian forces continued offensive operations northwest of Orikhiv on October 9 and 10 but did not advance as Ukrainian forces are attempting to infiltrate into central and eastern Orikhiv. [44]
Russian forces launched five glide bombs against Zaporizhzhia City on October 10, killing at least 17 people and injuring at least 16. Ukrainian officials, including Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, confirmed on October 10 that Russian forces launched five glide bombs against residential buildings in Zaporizhzhia City.[45] Russia’s recent intensification of strikes against civilian and critical infrastructure in Zaporizhzhia City intends to complicate civilian life within major population centers, introduce constant fear and uncertainty to daily life, degrade the Ukrainian economy, and degrade defenses in the city to facilitate future Russian offensives.[46]
Ukrainian forces continued their intermediate-range strike campaign against Russian military training areas in occupied Kherson Oblast. The Ukrainian General Staff reported that Ukrainian forces struck a Russian training center for non-commissioned officers (NCOs) near Khorly (roughly 74 kilometers from the frontline) on October 9 or overnight on October 9 to 10.[47]
Ukrainian long-range strikes against a railway bridge in Rostov Oblast on October 10 are causing train delays in occupied Crimea. A private Russian railway carrier, Grand Service Express, reported that all trains that travel from occupied Crimea to southern Russia are experiencing delays due to the damage to the railway bridge between Rostov-on-Don and Bataysk, Rostov Oblast.[48]
Russian Objective: Target Ukrainian military and civilian infrastructure in the rear and on the frontline
Note: The Ukrainian military ceased regularly reporting the numbers of Russian missiles fired and the number of missiles intercepted as of August 11. [49]
Russian forces conducted a series of drone strikes against Ukraine on the night of October 9 to 10, causing power outages that stopped public transport in much of Kyiv City. The Ukrainian Air Force reported that Russian forces launched 160 Shahed-type drones (including 110 jet-powered drones), Gerbera-type strike drones, and Parodiya-type decoy drones from the directions of Oryol and Kursk cities; Shatalovo, Smolensk Oblast; Millerovo, Rostov Oblast; Primorsko-Akhtarsk, Krasnodar Krai; occupied Donetsk City; and occupied Hvardiiske, Crimea.[50] The Ukrainian Air Force reported that Ukrainian forces downed 117 drones, including 82 jet-powered drones; that an unspecified number of drones hit 14 locations; and that drone debris fell at 10 locations. Ukrainian forces shot down a Russian jet-powered drone near Lviv City on the morning of October 10, which Russian and Ukrainian sources noted was the first instance of a Russian jet-powered drone entering Lviv City's airspace.[51] Ukrainian officials reported that train traffic and escalators at all three underground metro lines in Kyiv City suspended operations after Russian strikes caused a sudden loss of voltage from external electrical supply hubs, and that trolleybus and tram routes also suspended operations in parts of the capital due to substation outages.[52] Kyiv City Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko reported water supply disruptions in the city on October 10.[53]
Russia's Winter 2026-2027 strike campaign against Ukraine's energy grid is trying in part to damage Ukraine's infrastructure for transmitting electricity between regions. Ukrainian state energy company Ukrenergo reported on October 9 that Russian forces are using a new strike tactic to degrade Ukraine's energy grid — continuous strikes on substations, transmission lines, and distribution systems, especially near large cities that consume high quantities of electricity.[54] Russian forces are systematically destroying power generation facilities in some Ukrainian oblasts, leading to energy shortages in those areas and forcing Ukrainian authorities to transmit electricity from other regions hundreds of kilometers away. Ukrenergo noted that continuous Russian strikes and air raid sirens complicate efforts to rapidly repair and restore damaged energy infrastructure. A multi-part Russian plan for the Winter 2026-2027 strike campaign, which Western media outlets recently reviewed and publicized, also calls for Russian strikes to disable substations, transmission lines, and interconnectors near Ukraine's western border through which Ukraine imports European electricity.[55] Russia's Winter 2025-2026 strike campaign also targeted Ukraine's transmission and distribution infrastructure.[56]
Russian efforts to increase its military presence in Belarus and further integrate Belarus into Russian-favorable frameworks
