### 19. ★★★☆☆ How genocidal war on Gaza is shaping Israel’s election

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T05:35:09+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share How genocidal war on Gaza is shaping Israel’s election on social media
Ultra-Orthodox Jewish men stand next to a billboard depicting Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu alongside leaders of opposition parties, with the slogan 'Right or Palestine', before Israel's general election, in Ashdod, Israel [Ohad Zwigenberg/AP]
As Israel entered the final month before its October 27 election, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu had a chilling warning to share.
Israel’s enemies, he said on September 29, were plotting an attack on the country before the vote. Without providing any evidence, he then quickly referred to continuing attacks by Israeli troops in the Gaza Strip, where he said they had killed more than 100 “terrorists” in recent weeks.
That link drawn by Netanyahu – between the genocidal war on Gaza that began after the Hamas-led October 7, 2023 attacks, the upcoming elections, and Israel’s national security – was not coincidental, say analysts.
Netanyahu’s insistence that he alone can secure Israelis from a range of threats they face from across the country’s borders, they say, lies at the heart of his re-election campaign, even as his alleged failures during the attack three years ago and in securing the quick release of captives have offered his opponents ammunition.
When a Flydubai flight packed with Israeli passengers on their way from Dubai to Tel Aviv a day after his recent warning nearly crashed after a co-pilot attacked his pilot with an axe in the cockpit, Netanyahu was quick to seize on the incident to argue that he is still man for the country’s top job.
Polls suggest that he faces a stiff test in what is the first general vote since the 2023 attacks, which led to the killing of 1,139 people in Israel and triggered the genocidal war on the Gaza Strip, where Israeli have attacks have since killed more than 74,000 Palestinians.
Three years later, the war has become inseparable from the domestic debate over Israel’s direction while also accelerating a political shift to the right – with once-fringe positions on Palestinians now very much the mainstream view.
Few embody this transformation more than Israel’s far-right financial Minister Bezalel Smotrich, sanctioned by a number of Western countries and known for his incendiary and racist language and policies towards Palestinians.
In one of his characteristically inflammatory statements , Smotrich told Israeli outlet Ynet late last month that the Israeli army should “go to war” in the occupied West Bank and “do there what we did in Gaza”.
Less than two weeks later, Smotrich’s electoral ally Moshe Feiglin added fuel to the fire.
“The enemy is Gaza, not Hamas”, he told the Jerusalem Post, calling for the annexation of the besieged territory after the election. “The baby that will be born tomorrow in Gaza will do exactly the same,” he said, in reference to the 2023 attacks.
Palestinian political analyst Ahmed Najar said these are not “stray comments” from marginal figures but an electoral message from power-seeking politicians aimed at portraying Palestinians as “not a people with rights, but a demographic and security problem to be expelled, conquered or destroyed”.
He added: “The frightening reality is that the coming election risks becoming a competition not over whether Palestinian life should be protected, but over who can promise to erase it most aggressively.”
Following October 7, the younger generation in particular slid rightwards, said Ahron Bregman, an Israeli security analyst and senior teaching fellow at King’s College London.
He said the forces driving Israel to the right had been gathering for decades.
“The trend toward the right began after the Yom Kippur War in 1973, but it has intensified dramatically since October 7,” Bregman told Al Jazeera, referring to the bloody 19-day October War , or the 1973 Arab-Israeli War – a conflict between Israel and a coalition of Arab states led by Egypt and Syria.
More recently, the far right has been emboldened through electoral politics as part of Netanyahu’s government since 2022 – the most right-wing government in Israeli history.
Another outspoken cabinet member alongside Smotrich is Minister of National Security Itamar Ben-Gvir , a far-right provocateur who has previously been convicted of inciting racism and of supporting a terrorist organisation.
“Netanyahu’s greatest mistake – among many – was bringing figures like Ben-Gvir and Smotrich into his coalition,” said Bergman. “By doing so, he legitimised them as influential forces in Israeli politics.”
Ben-Gvir has been so cemented into Israel’s political system, he has hopes pinned on becoming the next Israeli defence minister, the analyst pointed out.
“This is madness. But that’s contemporary Israel,” said Bergman.
Attendees listen and applaud during an election campaign meeting on October 5, 2026 in Tel Aviv, Israel [Erik Marmor/Getty Images] Analysts also pointed out that several parties running, including ones that are currently in power and a number that are challenging the current coalition, all advocate for the expulsion of Palestinians from Gaza, as one of the examples of the shift to the right.
Omar Baddar, director of Palthink for Strategic Studies, said the messaging from across the Israeli political and military spectrum in the lead-up to the election shows that there is “a consensus” to continue the attacks on Gaza, where nearly 1,500 have been killed since a “ceasefire” last year.
“They’re using the threats to get more votes,” he said, pointing out to what he described as the radicalisation of the society.
With the election approaching, added Baddar, Israeli leaders are seeking to undo one another: “Who[ever]’s more radical is more likely to get more votes.”
As Ehab Jabareen, a Palestinian political analyst and researcher from inside Israel, writes , “what it means to be ‘right-wing’ in Israel today is above all to take a harder line on Palestinian national claims, security and territorial control.”
He adds: “On security and the Palestinians, a large part of Jewish Israeli politics already works from assumptions that would have been treated as right-wing a generation ago: Deep suspicion of a sovereign Palestinian state, a high tolerance for prolonged military control, and a stronger insistence on Jewish sovereignty as the starting point rather than one value among others.”
Observers and rights groups say the disparity is even evident in the electoral process. A prominent politician and Palestinian citizen of Israel, Sami Abu Shehadeh, has been forced to withdraw from the election over his remarks about the Gaza war, while Israeli far-right leaders have remained untouched despite their violent rhetoric against Palestinians.
Recent polling indicates that the events of October 7 are top of mind for Israelis, who also have questions about who was responsible for the security failures of that day.
Just last month, reports surfaced indicating that Netanyahu had received two separate warnings about an imminent Hamas attack in 2023, from Egypt’s intelligence chief, Abbas Kamel, and United Arab Emirates President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan.
Netanyahu’s two main rivals in the race for prime minister have cited the alleged warnings to call the prime minister “unfit” to remain at the helm of the country.
But Netanyahu has denied suggestions that he had been forewarned about the attack, and the reports do not appear to have had a significant effect on opinion polls.
While October 7 represents a profound rupture in Israelis’ sense of security, public trust in the Netanyahu government had already been badly shaken by its controversial judicial overhaul , introduced when the prime minister’s current coalition first came to power, said Mairav Zonszein, an Israel-Palestine analyst at the International Crisis Group based in Tel Aviv.
Despite these breaches of trust, the Israeli leader retains support.
“Israelis in general still see Netanyahu as kind of the guy on security. And more to the point, they don’t really see an alternative,” Zonszein told Al Jazeera.
A June survey by Mitvim – the Israeli Institute for Regional Foreign Policies found that the Israeli public supports its government’s military and security solutions, and rejects resolving the Palestinian issue and ending the Israeli occupation of Palestinian territory. This is the case even while it has been concerned about international isolation and the decline of Israel’s international standing since the beginning of the genocidal war on Gaza, the survey reported.
Yet Israelis are also increasingly war-weary, even as they back the use of more force. Its troops – stretched between six fronts of war in the last few years – are bearing the brunt of this fatigue in Israeli society.
Israel’s Ministry of Defence said on Tuesday that nearly 30,000 soldiers and security personnel are facing psychological trauma since the war.
The ministry said its rehabilitation department’s budget has increased by 88 percent over the past three years to 10 billion shekels ($3.3m), with the majority supporting treatment for mental health conditions and post-traumatic stress disorder.
Still, Israel in recent months has been more and more vocal about its expansionist vision, with officials brazenly stating the goal of “ Greater Israel ”. The claim for the conquest is based on a biblical verse (Genesis 15:18-21).
For decades, Israel has ignored international law in its continuous occupation of Palestinian and Syrian land, and has shown little respect for its neighbours’ sovereignty, occupying more land in Syria, as well as in Lebanon in more recent years.
According to Najar, the political economist, the “complete dehumanisation” of Palestinians as evidenced during the campaign reveal that Gaza “is being presented to Israeli voters not as a catastrophe or a crime for which there must be accountability, but as a blueprint to be repeated elsewhere”.
In southern Lebanon, where Israel has been carrying out near daily attacks and and has occupied large swathes of land, residents have told Al Jazeera they expect little to change even if Netanyahu is defeated in the election.
Netanyahu’s main political challenger, Gadi Eisenkot – head of the Yashar party and former army chief of staff – is the father of the so-called Dahiyeh Doctrine, and is just as hawkish about expansion. That doctrine, since Israel’s war with the Lebanese group Hezbollah in 2006, calls for the use of huge force, even against civilian infrastructure, as a deterrent against enemies and was deployed in Israel’s recent wars, particularly in Beirut’s southern suburbs.
If it were up to Eisenkot, the Lebanon front of war would have never scaled down: He has accused Netanyahu of bowing to US pressure to halt operations in Beirut, and has insisted that the military should be able to attack Hezbollah anywhere in Lebanon.
For Israelis still reeling from the events of October 7, Eisenkot could fill the security void many are feeling, said Zonszein.
“They really … see the world as kind of very hostile towards them, the world not understanding what they went through on October 7, and [therefore why] the need to have these security buffer zones in Lebanon, in Syria, and Gaza,” she said.
A two-state solution to the Israel-Palestine conflict is therefore out of the question, said the analysts – and has not even made a blip of an appearance in campaigning before the election.
The idea first emerged during the Oslo Accords more than 30 years ago, which set out a negotiating process aimed at resolving the Israeli-Palestinian conflict, predicated on the belief that the creation of a Palestinian state alongside Israel was imperative to forge peace.
But since the Oslo process began in 1993, the number of Israeli settlers in the West Bank has ballooned from about 260,000 to more than 700,000, embedded deep inside West Bank areas that would be needed for a contiguous Palestinian state.
“The concept of a two-state solution is currently irrelevant and likely will remain so for another decade or two, if not longer,” said Bergman. “It is unlikely to become a reality in the foreseeable future, as no Israeli leader can propose it to the Israeli public after the events of October 7.”
The only way it could regain prominence is if there is substantial international pressure, including severe sanctions on Israel, and a non-violent intifada, or an uprising by Palestinians, he said.
The outright rejection of Palestinian statehood has simply become much more “acceptable”, said Zonszein.
“There’s several parties who advocate these views, whereas there isn’t a single Jewish Zionist Party that puts the need for a two-state solution or a Palestinian political process at the forefront,” she said.
Taken all together, Israel’s election appears less a demand for change than a further entrenchment of the jingoism that surged after October 7, 2023.
“[There’s] more and more movement towards more military force, more need for pushing the borders … out of a lot of fear that another October 7 could happen,” said Zonszein.

### 20. ★★★☆☆ Pentagon Again Increases Number of U.S. Casualties in Iran War - The New York Times

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T22:44:06+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Pentagon updates official count of US service members killed during Iran war - report
The Washington Post reported in September that the previous official count of 18 may have been inaccurate and that at least four service members were potentially excluded from the official tally.
US President Donald Trump and Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth salute as a US Army carry team carries the flag-draped transfer case containing remains during a dignified transfer event for US service members killed in the Middle East, at Dover Air Force Base in Dover, Delaware, on July 22, 2026.
( photo credit : SAUL LOEB / AFP via Getty Images )
The Pentagon officially updated its count of US service members who have been killed in connection with Operation Epic Fury and the ongoing war with Iran to 21 from the previous figure of 18, according to a Saturday report from The Washington Post .
The update to the US’s Defense Casualty Analysis System (DCAS) included the two noncombat deaths of Army National Guard Maj. Sorffly Davius, who died in Kuwait in March, and an unidentified Navy suicide, an anonymous US official told The Washington Post .
The Washington Post reported in September that the previous official count of 18 may have been inaccurate and that at least four service members were potentially excluded from the DCAS tally, at the time citing six US officials familiar with the Pentagon’s internal casualty accounting data.
Following the update, 15 deaths are included in the DCAS count for service members killed during Operation Epic Fury, and an additional six are listed as military deaths during overseas operations.
According to The Washington Post , the category of military deaths during overseas operations was created after US President Donald Trump declared that Operation Epic Fury had officially ended, despite ongoing hostilities and exchanges of strikes.
In this handout photo provided by the US Navy, An F/A-18F Super Hornet, attached to Strike Fighter Squadron (VFA) 41, flies over the flight deck of Nimitz-class aircraft carrier USS Abraham Lincoln (CVN 72) in support of Operation Epic Fury on March 6, 2026 in the Mediterranean Sea; illustrative (credit: Handout Photo by the US Navy via Getty Images)
Hegseth labeled reports of inaccurate death count a 'complete lie'
In September, US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth decried The Washington Post ’s report that the official Pentagon death count was inaccurate as “a complete lie.”
In a post on X/Twitter at the time, Hegseth said The Washington Post was “worse than Iranian state media.”
US House Armed Services Committee Ranking Member Adam Smith and Military Personnel Subcommittee Ranking Member Chrissy Houlahan, who previously urged the US Department of Defense to look into the discrepancies in casualty numbers, released a statement on Friday responding to the correction.
“There is no excuse for a mistake of this magnitude,” they wrote, adding that “requiring Congress to step in to rectify such an egregious error is an embarrassment for the Department [of Defense].”
They stated that they are glad to see that the incorrect count had been updated, but vowed to continue to fight for transparency, accountability, and procedures to ensure such miscounts do not happen in the future.
The one email that makes sense of the Middle East. Iran, the Gulf, Syria, Turkey, and whatever moved that day, from The Jerusalem Post, every evening.

### 21. ★★★☆☆ Putin relayed Iran war proposal to Trump, Kremlin says - CNBC

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T13:41:46+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Putin relayed Iran war proposal to Trump, Kremlin says
Russian President Vladimir Putin conveyed Tehran&#x27;s view on a possible resolution of the Iran conflict to U.S. President Donald Trump, Russian state media reported.
The report comes a day after Trump said Russia will supply diesel to the global market amid a surge in energy prices caused by the wars in Iran and Ukraine.
Trump said Russia will supply more than 4 million tons of diesel to the global market under an arrangement Trump said he agreed with Putin.
U.S. President Donald Trump (R) greets Russian President Vladimir Putin as he arrives at Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson on August 15, 2025 in Anchorage, Alaska.
Russian President Vladimir Putin conveyed Tehran&#x27;s vision for a possible end to the war in Iran to U.S. President Donald Trump , Russian state media reported Saturday.
The report comes a day after Trump said Russia will supply diesel to the global market amid a surge in energy prices caused by the wars in Iran and Ukraine.
Russia&#x27;s Interfax news agency quoted Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov as saying Putin sent the message to Trump "in agreement with Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian," according to a Google translation.
Other Russian media reports said Putin spoke to Trump by  phone  after the Russian leader met Pezeshkian on the sidelines of a summit in Turkmenistan.
Interfax didn&#x27;t elaborate on the specifics of how Iran sees the war — which began on Feb. 28 with U.S. and Israeli airstrikes on Iranian targets — coming to an end.
The White House didn&#x27;t immediately respond to CNBC&#x27;s emailed request to confirm the reported conversation between Putin and Trump.
Trump said Friday that Russia will supply more than 4 million tons of diesel to the global market under an arrangement the U.S. president said he agreed with Putin during a phone call.
Russia will immediately supply more than 300,000 tons of diesel, followed by 500,000 tons in November, and 1 million tons immediately thereafter, Trump said in a post on Truth Social . Moscow will then deliver another 3 million tons of diesel based on the condition of Russia&#x27;s refineries, Trump said.
The Treasury Department temporarily waived sanctions on Russian diesel through April 2027 under a general license issued Friday.
Brent crude oil price, U.S. dollars per barrel, year to date
Iran has stepped up its attacks on oil tankers transiting the Strait of Hormuz, with vessels coming under fire almost daily as Tehran tries to choke off a rebound in crude exports.
A senior Iranian Revolutionary Guard official said Wednesday that Iran will block all "illicit routes" through Hormuz, according to the Fars News Agency , an outlet considered close to the Guard.
The surge in attacks on tankers comes as crude oil exports from the Middle East rebounded in September to prewar levels, largely because the U.S. military escorted ships through Hormuz along Oman&#x27;s coast.
An interim deal signed in June between the U.S. and Iran to pause hostilities to allow for negotiations quickly broke down.
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy immediately condemned Trump&#x27;s diesel deal with Putin. Ukraine&#x27;s leader warned that easing sanctions without a commitment from Russia to de-escalate the war will only prolong it.
"Gifts to Putin will not bring peace or any benefit to the civilized world," Zelenskyy said in a social media post . "Russia will &#x27;repay&#x27; the diesel with further terror and perfidy. Allowing Russia to sell petroleum products is an investment in a war that must be ended, not prolonged."
Diesel prices have surged worldwide as Ukraine has pounded Russian refineries, forcing Moscow to ban diesel exports to global markets. Iran and its Houthi allies have also attacked refineries in the Middle East, further constraining fuel supplies.
Trump is under growing political pressure to lower fuel prices ahead of the November midterm elections. Republicans face competitive elections in conservative strongholds like Iowa, where farmers feel the pinch of high diesel prices.

### 23. ★★★☆☆ Young men live in fear of being snatched off the streets and sold to Myanmar's army

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T22:13:08+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, Authorities in Myanmar have been ordered to aggressively fulfil the military&#x27;s recruitment quotas, leading to corruption, extortion and abductions
You do not stay out after dark if you&#x27;re young and able-bodied in Hpa-An, the capital of Myanmar&#x27;s southern Karen state. In recent months this has become conventional wisdom.
"I worry, but I have to go out to work," says the 26-year-old tuk tuk driver, who asked that his real name be omitted out of fear for his safety. "My family call me all the time and remind me not to drive passengers too far; to be careful who I take as a passenger... I have to be vigilant all the time.
"People are panicked and frightened," he explains. "Some people I know have been taken."
Locals say plain-clothed members and supporters of the Myanmar junta have been roaming the streets of Hpa-An, arbitrarily arresting people in their teens, 20s and 30s and forcing them into military service.
According to leaked documents seen by BBC Burmese, authorities have been ordered to aggressively fulfil the military&#x27;s recruitment quotas across jurisdictions, bolstering the junta&#x27;s ranks as it endures a war of attrition against ethnic militias, pro-democracy groups and civilian-led resistance movements.
Zaw Min is the only one among his siblings who is at the age of recruitment. Many other "kids" his age have fled, he says – to Thailand, or the homes of relatives further afield. Those who remain are often under strict instructions from their parents: be home before dusk.
Myanmar has been locked in a bloody civil war since February 2021, when its own military launched a coup d&#x27;etat and ousted democratically elected leader Aung San Suu Kyi.
Three years later, following a string of humiliating losses, plummeting morale and soaring rates of desertion, the junta enacted a mandatory conscription law that required all men aged 18 to 35 and women aged 18 to 27 to serve at least two years under military command. Those who refuse face up to five years in prison.
The enactment of the law "represents one of the most severe and impactful actions taken against the entire civilian population since the military coup", according to Maj Naung Yoe, military affairs official at the Myanmar Defense and Security Institute (MDSI) and a former military officer who defected and joined the anti-coup Civil Disobedience Movement.
Image caption, An estimated 100,000 young recruits have been conscriptedby the junta since early 2024
Hpa-An is the latest to be caught in a dragnet that&#x27;s already ensnared an estimated 100,000 young recruits over the past two-and-a-half years – many of them via coercion, extortion and abductions.
"Security forces track young people&#x27;s movements to arrest them, force them into service under the pretext of statutory military eligibility, extort money through threats of detention, and arrest individuals during guest-list inspections," Naung Yoe says.
The BBC contacted the Myanmar government&#x27;s Central Body for Summoning People&#x27;s Military Servants regarding incidents mentioned in this story, including the forced recruitment of conscripts and reports of abductions allegedly carried out in recent months.
With the mandatory conscription law, the junta has established a human supply chain to sustain its armed forces. And it appears to have worked. Observers note that reinforcements have played a critical role in helping the regime claw back more territory. In many parts of Myanmar, the resistance is now on the back foot.
The war, though, is far from over. And as more youths evade conscription – either to join the resistance or simply avoid armed conflict against their fellow countrymen – the junta&#x27;s recruitment methods have become ever more extreme.
International rights groups such as Amnesty International, Human Rights Watch and Fortify Rights say Myanmar&#x27;s military is abducting civilians and forcing them into military service.
In July, open-source research organisation Myanmar Witness revealed that they had analysed hundreds of missing-person advertisements collected from Facebook between 2021 and 2025.
Researchers found that missing person notices for January rose from four in 2021, pre-coup, to 82 in 2025, post-conscription law.
"In practice, much of [the Myanmar military&#x27;s] recruitment appears to be carried out through detention, coercion, and disappearance rather than formal call-up, and often without notice to families," they concluded.
Men and boys constituted the majority of reported missing persons.
Figure caption, Watch: BBC goes into jungle with Myanmar&#x27;s civil war rebels
"They are spreading rumours all over the neighbourhood that I sold my husband."
Ma Moe San Pan sobs the words through gulps of air. She is sitting in her kitchen, at home in Yangon, where on the morning of 8 May 2025 she saw her 33-year-old husband Phyo Wai for the last time.
"We had an argument, and afterwards he left home," she recalls."I was upset and told my cousin about what had happened."
Ma Moe San Pan&#x27;s cousin worked at the local ward administration office, and she asked him to bring her husband back. It was two days later that Phyo Wai called her, from a military recruitment centre on the other side of the city.
The cousin had taken him to the ward office which had sold him on to another ward for 4 million kyats ($900; £680) to fill that ward&#x27;s recruitment quota, Ma Moe San Pan says. The BBC was unable to reach Ma Moe San Pan&#x27;s cousin.
In a desperate attempt to bring him back, Ma Moe San Pan was then scammed out of 4.5 million kyats by strangers who falsely promised his release.
U Maung Maung Lwin, the administrator of the ward to which Phyo Wai was taken, later admitted to the BBC that he had played a role in sending him to the military recruitment authorities.
When asked about allegations that individuals were being conscripted outside their home township, he said transfers from other townships do occur when local quotas cannot be met.
But he denied that any money was involved in such cases, or in Phyo Wai&#x27;s forced conscription.
"[Phyo Wai] told me they said they would help him and made him sign documents," Ma Moe San Pan recalls.
"If my husband had been legally selected for conscription through the normal process, I could have accepted that. What I cannot accept is that our own ward administration office allegedly sold him to another ward.
Image caption, Ma Moe San Pan says her husband was sold into military service by her own cousin
In the time since Phyo Wai was taken, Ma Moe San Pan has filed police complaints relating to two matters: the sale of her husband into military service, and the 4.5 million kyats she lost to scammers.
The police accepted her complaint, she says, but refused her request to include human trafficking charges, claiming a human trafficking case could only be registered if a person had been sold abroad.
Ma Moe San Pan&#x27;s case highlights a grim mutation of the junta&#x27;s mandatory conscription laws: mounting pressure from the higher levels of the regime has created perverse incentives further down the chain of command.
Surging demand and dwindling supply have fuelled an illicit market for eligible recruits. And with money on the table, security officials have taken to arresting youth at road checkpoints, tracking and ambushing workers during their daily commutes, setting traps via fake job advertisements, and forcibly entering people&#x27;s homes, Naung Yoe says.
"Under the guise of the conscription law, these coercive arrests and recruitment drives have effectively transformed into a modern-day slavery and human trafficking enterprise."
In late August, police in Yangon arrested a member of the Fire Services Department – a branch of the home affairs ministry – and his son-in-law for abducting four young men with the intention of selling them for military conscription.
The reserve fire brigade unit commander allegedly deceived the men with promises of employment opportunities, luring them to his house and trapping them in a series of purpose-built detention rooms. Police officers raided the residence and arrested him on 25 August. He has since been charged with kidnapping or abducting in order to wrongfully confine a person, and faces up to 7 years in prison.
BBC Burmese made several attempts to contact the police station where he is being held, but was unsuccessful.
"Lower-level administrative bodies, military personnel and local criminal gangs employ diverse tactics to press-gang and arrest young people," Naung Yoe says.
"They sell these individuals to military units and regional commands facing manpower shortages, or traffic substitute recruits on behalf of wealthier individuals seeking to buy their way out of service."
Image caption, It&#x27;s been more than a year since Ma Moe San Pan last saw her husband Phyo Wai
While conscription orders are indiscriminate on paper – demanding a quota of recruits from each village, ward, or township proportional to its population – wealthier conscripts can simply pay to dodge the draft and be replaced by someone more financially vulnerable.
So it is the poor who disproportionately bear the burden.
Naung Yoe, meanwhile, told the BBC that "these practices are escalating due to increasing difficulties in meeting monthly recruitment quotas and the lucrative financial profits made from recruit trading at the ground level".
But the hardening fist of forced conscription also reveals the junta&#x27;s empty hand, and its desperation for manpower.
It "seems to be another step in the junta&#x27;s efforts to enlist unwilling youth in a conflict that underscores its embattled position in the civil war" says Dr Sandeep Singh, a research fellow who teaches military studies at the National University of Singapore.
The climate surrounding routine abductions, he adds, has "generated further difficulties on the youth of Myanmar, already affected by years of civil war, and their families".
In addition to the daily anxiety of life in Myanmar, many would-be recruits now fear being forced to fight in a deadly conflict that they not only don&#x27;t believe in, but actively oppose. This constant threat has fuelled massive internal displacement and severe labour shortages in an already flailing economy, and sent people fleeing to neighbouring countries.
Having spent more than half a decade waging war against its own population, the junta is now wielding the conscription law as yet another tool of subjugation, observers say.
As the MDSI put it in a statement published in June: "The generals have benefited by using military law to make the citizens fight against each other."
Millions opposed military rule in Myanmar. Is their dream of a revolution over?
From behind bars, Aung San Suu Kyi casts a long shadow over Myanmar
Soldier-spies in Myanmar help pro-democracy rebels make crucial gains
The college scam that promised students fleeing war a new life in Finland

### 24. ★★★☆☆ Expedition hopes to find aviator Amelia Earhart's plane on remote island

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T18:22:29+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, The research team departed Kiribati&#x27;s capital on Wednesday and hope to arrive at the suspected wreck site by Monday
A team of researchers are on their way to a remote island to inspect what they think could be the plane wreckage of famed aviator Amelia Earhart.
The group, from the US-based Archaeological Legacy Institute (ALI) and Purdue University, are expected to arrive at Nikumaroro Island in the Pacific Ocean by Monday in the hopes of solving the mystery of her disappearance.
The American pioneer vanished while attempting to circumnavigate the globe in 1937, spawning decades of speculation on her fate.
The team are aiming to find what has been called the Taraia Object, first spotted sitting on the edge of a lagoon off Nikumaroro in satellite imagery in 2020.
Purdue University said the object had since been confirmed to have been visible in aerial photography of the area dating back to 1938.
The researchers are currently roughly halfway through their journey to the remote atoll aboard the Marin Mainiku, a Kiribati National Shipping Line vessel.
They departed the capital of Kiribati on Wednesday to begin the 830-mile journey to Nikumaroro, which is also part of the multi-island country.
When they eventually arrive, they will have to navigate through a narrow gap created in the atoll&#x27;s reef by the British in 1963 - the last time the island was inhabited.
"Finding Amelia Earhart&#x27;s Electra aircraft would be the discovery of a lifetime," Richard Pettigrew, head of the ALI and the expedition lead, said.
Image caption, A satellite image of the so-called Taraia Object in a lagoon on Nikumaroro
Earhart had already successfully flown from California across South America, Africa and Asia in her Electra twin-engine plane when she arrived in Lae, Papua New Guinea on 2 July 1937.
From there, she and her navigator Fred Noonan had planned to travel towards Hawaii and then finally back to California.
But her plane vanished without a trace while trying to reach Howland Island, a remote, uninhabited coral island near Nikumaroro, to refuel.
Earhart&#x27;s disappearance spawned numerous theories - from a simple crash due to fuel exhaustion to more elaborate claims of interference.
The official explanation is that the plane suffered communication problems while the two struggled to find the island, and Earhart eventually crashed into the ocean when she ran out of fuel.
While this version of events is largely accepted, no physical evidence has ever been recovered.
Image caption, Amelia Earhart disappeared over the Pacific Ocean while piloting her Electra plane in 1937
One theory is that Earhart unintentionally navigated to Nikumaroro, where she ditched her plane and died a castaway.
Images of the so-called Taraia Object show what could be the remnants of a small aircraft sitting in shallow waters.
But some - including rival explorers - have expressed scepticism that this could be a plane, let alone Earhart&#x27;s.
Ric Gillespie, who specialises in recovering historic aircraft wrecks, told news agency Reuters that the object looked like a tree that had fallen from the atoll into the nearby water.
"I&#x27;d love to find that airplane. It ain&#x27;t gonna happen," he said.
The researchers en route to the island credit Gillespie with previously finding evidence of a human presence on the atoll in the 1930s and forensic analysis of skeletal remains found there in the 1940s they say lend credence to their theory.
But Gillespie suspects Earhart&#x27;s plane had likely broken apart and its fragments carried into the depths of the Pacific long ago.
Previous attempts to find her Electra have also come up short.
In 2024, a group of researchers said they may have found it lying around 4,877m (16,000ft) below the surface near Howland Island through sonar imaging - but this was later confirmed to be a rock formation.
Similarly, an expedition in 2019, conducted by the marine archaeologist who discovered the Titanic wreck, to investigate a photograph of what might have been a landing gear protruding from the water near Nikumaroro also failed to find anything.

### 25. ★★★☆☆ SCOTUS weighs local governments’ climate change lawsuits against oil, gas companies - The North State Journal

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: GN Energy / Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T19:25:28+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Climate activists gather outside the Supreme Court as the nation’s high court hears arguments in Suncor Energy v. Boulder County on Monday in Washington, D.C. (Manuel Balce Ceneta / AP
WASHINGTON, D.C. — The Supreme Court grappled Monday with whether local governments can sue oil and gas companies in state court to help cover costs associated with climate change, an issue with potentially billions of dollars at stake.
Key justices appeared wary about the potential sweep of litigation but torn over the complex questions of federal and state law raised by the case.
Officials in Boulder argue their lawsuit is an attempt to hold energy companies Suncor and ExxonMobil accountable for their contributions to climate change.
They say that worsening disasters such as floods and wildfires put a heavy financial burden on local communities, and the industry should bear part of that cost.
The city and county of Boulder first sued the oil companies in 2018, years before the 2021 Marshall Fire destroyed nearly 1,100 homes and left two people dead. Total damage was estimated at $2 billion, making it the costliest wildfire in Colorado history.
Boulder case part of wave of legal action against oil companies
Governments from largely blue-leaning areas around the country have filed similar lawsuits seeking damages that could total billions of dollars, part of a wave of legal action worldwide seeking to leverage action through the courts.
“Presumably, if you prevail, the next day, a municipality in every single state will file a lawsuit,” said Chief Justice John Roberts. “How do you think that will work out on the ground?”
Justice Brett Kavanaugh said a win by Boulder could bankrupt the oil companies and other potential defendants.
“I mean, anyone is a potential defendant for a suit like this. Any manufacturer, any business,” he said.
A win for the oil and gas companies at the Supreme Court, meanwhile, could shut down dozens of similar cases filed in recent years.
Suncor and Exxon argue that the Clean Air Act gives the federal government — not the states — the power to regulate emissions.
They have the support of the Trump administration, which argues the lawsuits are an unconstitutional attempt to regulate planet-warming emissions and impose Colorado’s state law beyond its borders.
But Roberts and other justices pointed out that state courts consider lawsuits about myriad issues that have nationwide reach.
“I guess I’m not quite sure what makes this situation different from those,” Roberts said.
The court is also weighing a jurisdictional question that could allow it an “off-ramp” to avoid deciding the issues right away. Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson seemed like a vote in favor of that path.
“Why shouldn’t we wait and hear all of these claims once the state courts are done?” she asked.
The case was heard without Justice Samuel Alito, who recused himself just a week before arguments after facing calls to step aside because he owns stock in oil companies. That raises the possibility of a tie vote.
A deadlock would leave in place a decision from Colorado’s highest court allowing the case to proceed.
Attorney Kannon Shanmugam, representing the industry, urged the justices to reverse that ruling. He said the Boulder case was attempting to use the courts to decide a public policy issue that is properly up to Congress and other elected officials.
“Climate change is certainly one of the most pressing issues” facing the country, but the lawsuit “is exactly the wrong way to go about” addressing it, he said.
Principal Deputy Solicitor General Sarah Harris said Boulder’s case “egregiously exceeds” the state’s authority. The county essentially declares itself a victim of actions that occurred largely out of state, she said.
But Kevin Russell, an attorney representing Boulder, argued that state lawsuits can address damage in Colorado, regardless of where the fossil fuels are produced.
Boulder is not trying to regulate greenhouse gas emissions or stop the companies from continuing to produce oil and gas, Russell said. Instead, officials are seeking to hold the companies accountable for allegedly misleading the public about their contribution to global warming by burning fossil fuels, he said.
“Chapter 3” of case trilogy, after tobacco and opioids
Justice Elena Kagan compared the climate case to previous state-based lawsuits against the tobacco industry and opioid makers.
“This is Chapter 3,” she said, noting the similarities of states claiming harms from products produced nationally and even internationally.
“This is a different book,” replied Shanmugam, the industry lawyer. Harms claimed by Boulder “are diffused nationwide and indeed worldwide,” he said.
Trump’s Republican administration has rolled back environmental regulations, including a move last month by the Environmental Protection Agency to repeal rules that limit emissions from power plants fueled by coal and natural gas. States and cities have already sued over the repeal of the power plant rule.

### 27. ★★★☆☆ Like Meta's Muse AI Agents? 4 AI Semiconductor Stocks Set to Benefit From the Rise of AI Agents. - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T15:50:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Like Meta's Muse AI Agents? 4 AI Semiconductor Stocks Set to Benefit From the Rise of AI Agents.
Meta Platforms ' (NASDAQ: META) artificial intelligence (AI) Muse agents have taken the world by storm. After being introduced just last month, Muse already has roughly 2 million daily active users as of Oct. 5 and sits atop the most downloaded apps on Apple 's (NASDAQ: AAPL) App Store. With consumer, enterprise, and small-business AI agents, the agentic AI market is set to explode.
But before AI agents take over the world, they will need many more central processing units (CPUs) to power them. While graphics processing units (GPUs) are used to train AI models and run inference , what they are not good at is providing the sequential logic required by AI agents.
Missed AI's "Act 1"? Act 2 Could Be 14x Bigger. Most investors think they missed the AI boat because they didn't buy Nvidia in 2005. But according to our analysts, we're only at the end of "Act 1"—the R&D phase. "Act 2" is the global rollout. Continue »
That is where server CPUs come in. As such, the ratio of GPUs to CPUs is expected to drop from 8:1 for AI model training and 4:1 for inference to 1:1 for servers dedicated to AI agents.
Image source: Getty Images. As a result, Bank of America has projected that the data center CPU market will grow from around $61.4 billion this year to $210.6 billion in 2030. That is pretty consistent with the $220 billion projections that both Nvidia (NASDAQ: NVDA) and Advanced Micro Devices (NASDAQ: AMD) have placed on the server CPU market.
Let's look at the four semiconductor stocks set to benefit the most from the agentic AI uprising.
The company set to benefit most from the rise of agentic AI and the surge in server CPU demand is arguably Arm Holdings (NASDAQ: ARM). It derives revenue from the server CPU market in two ways. First, it provides the IP (intellectual property) through licensing and subscriptions for nearly all custom CPUs. Meanwhile, earlier this year, it announced it would start making its own server CPUs for the first time.
Bank of America projects that custom Arm-based chips can capture nearly 38% of the server CPU market in 2030, while Arm's new mass-market chip will take a 9.4% market share.
On the downside, the company is also highly tied to the smartphone market, which is seeing unit declines due to price increases related to higher component costs, specifically memory. However, its server CPU market should more than make up for this.
AMD is one of the leaders in the server CPU market, having consistently taken share from rival Intel (NASDAQ: AMD) over the past few years. The company has already designed high-performance CPUs with high core counts specifically for agentic AI workloads and looks well-positioned to continue taking market share.
While the company has said it is looking to capture a 50% market share, Bank of America estimates AMD's market share will climb from 27.7% this year to 30.7% in 2030.
AMD also has a big opportunity in AI inference with its GPUs, which should be a second big growth driver.
Intel (NASDAQ: INTC) is the current market share leader in the data center CPU space with an over 40% share in 2025. As such, it has seen a sudden burst of revenue growth, given that the market is supply-constrained. This has allowed it to sell older-generation CPUs and also raise prices.
However, Bank of America projects that its market share will drop to around 22% by 2030. It also has a money-losing foundry business that has been like a weight around the company's neck. After some huge gains this year, Intel is my least favorite way to play the current CPU megatrend.
While best known for its GPUs, Nvidia has also developed its own Arm-based CPUs. It sells these as stand-alone chips, packaged with its GPUs, and as parts of entire server racks.
While this positions Nvidia to get its fair share of this booming market, its GPU revenue ultimately overwhelms its CPU opportunity. Expect GPUs and complete end-to-end server systems to be the main drivers of the company's growth moving forward. It's still a great stock, but just one that benefits a little less from this opportunity, given the difference in size between the GPU and CPU markets.

### 28. ★★★☆☆ Couples looking to have children often turn to surrogacy agencies in Georgia without understanding the hidden costs

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: DW World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T07:45:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: After Russia's invasion of Ukraine, much of the surrogacy industry shifted to Georgia Image: SWR Advertisement
Three years ago, Olga, 40, and Daniel (both names changed to protect the their&#160;identities) from Hamburg decided to employ the services of a surrogate mother. But surrogacy is illegal in Germany, and people whose children are born through surrogacy must pursue the legal recognition of their parenthood in German courts.
Olga is a German citizen, but was born and raised in Russia . That's why the couple wanted to learn more about the surrogacy market in Russia. But foreign customers have been banned from signing Russian surrogacy contracts since 2022. After Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine , many international agencies that liaise between surrogates and intended parents moved to Georgia . So, Olga and Daniel traveled there.
In the past years, Georgia, a mountainous country in the Caucasus region, has become a sort of surrogacy hub, with women traveling from all over the world to work as surrogates. According to Georgia's Public Service Development Agency, over 3,000 surrogate mothers were registered in 2023 across the country. Since 2012, they've given birth to over 5,000 children. And since the beginning of Russia's war in Ukraine, the number of children born to surrogate mothers has skyrocketed. While an estimated 300 surrogate mothers gave birth in 2021, that number leapt to over 1,100 by 2023.
Anastasia, 39, is a surrogate mother from Russia working in Georgia. She has a grown daughter and has already seen through three surrogate pregnancies. Her first was in 2021, which she used to finance a property down payment.
Surrogacy in the US: a different path to parenthood
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
Anastasia says she completed her first two surrogacies in Russia, and her third — for a Japanese couple, brokered through the branch office of a Moscow clinic — in Georgia. Like many surrogate mothers, she went home to Russia after the embryo was implanted, and returned to Georgia when she was 22 weeks pregnant.
"I live in an apartment with another surrogate mother who carried twins to term. This program was the easiest and most practical one for me, but maybe I just got lucky with the right clinic," Anastasia told DW. "Other women who gave birth in Georgia told me they had difficulties. For example, after giving birth, they had to wait a long time before they were paid." She says she's ready to become a surrogate mother again.
Estimates vary, but there are about 19 clinics that offer such services across Georgia. And at around €45,000 ($50,400) to €55,000, the average service costs for agencies in Tbilisi or Batumi are only about a third of what agencies in other countries charge.
Several surrogate mothers who gave birth in Georgian clinics told DW that their average salary was between €18,000 and €28,000, with premiums added for birthing twins, for example.
Daniel and Olga told DW that they spent months looking for surrogacy agency in Georgia. "Pretty soon, we had access to a list of women from Russia, Ukraine, Kazakhstan, and Georgia, and were given a detailed description of the process, along with the contact information of lawyers who could help take care of the formalities," they recount. "We were a little concerned that not every agency could give clear answers&#160;about the health of some of the surrogate mothers."
Both work in the medical field, and Olga says perhaps some of their questions about the physical health and possible genetic disorders might have been too probing. "We picked a 30-year-old woman from Ukraine who had already been a surrogate mother once for a European couple," she says. The Russian-Georgian agency set up half-hour long Zoom call, "but that hardly seemed enough to make a final decision," Olga adds.
In the spring of 2024, the married couple traveled to Georgia's capital to meet Elena (her name has been changed to protect her identity). Following a long discussion, the agency had seemed reluctant when they agreed to bring her to Tbilisi for the meeting. Elena made a good first impression on the couple from Hamburg, showing them pictures of her children and telling them she wanted to use the money she earned to renovate her apartment.
"They had initially told us that the surrogate would spent the entire pregnancy living in a rented apartment in Tbilisi," Olga and Daniel tell DW. "But then we found out that Elena was going back home for a few months after the embryo was implanted, back to Ukraine, where there's war."
Georgian laws have a weak spot, the human rights lawyer Nino Bogveradze tells DW: They only regulate the relationship between the surrogate mothers, the intended&#160;parents, and the clinics, but they do not consider the roles that agencies play.&#160;
In Georgia, surrogate mothers have limited rights. Most concerns related to their pregnancy, medical care or payment are not regulated by national law but in contracts between the involved parties. And the state does not set minimum standards for social guarantees or contractual terms.
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
After an embryo has been implanted, surrogate mothers are not allowed to keep the child, leave the program, or end the pregnancy. Several human rights groups, including Human Rights Watch and the International Women's Health Coalition say the established practice in Georgia violates women's rights to reproductive self-determination.
"Agencies often speak of their special mission to help people have the child they've been dreaming of," Olga Doletskaya from the University of Leeds tell DW. "But in reality, they're primarily driven by financial interests. And that's why they exploit the precarious situation of surrogate mothers, and pressure intended&#160;parents."
Olga and Daniel from Hamburg agree. "We were constantly being made to understand that we should decide as quickly as possible. We had to hurry because apparently several other couples were also interested in our surrogate," Olga says.
And the agency kept stressing that it had paid out of pocket for bringing the potential surrogate mother to Georgia, despite that not being contractually agreed. "At some point, a representative noticed my emotional state and asked me how long I wanted to remain childless," Olga recounts. "I felt like I was being manipulated."
The couple returned to Germany with mixed feelings and decided to shelve the idea of employing a surrogate mother — at least, for now.
Mark Sheider and Asja Lokina contributed to this article, which originally appeared in Russian.

### 29. ★★★☆☆ Ukrainian prisoners of war and defectors have been gathered into a Russian battalion to face Ukrainian forces — why? And how?

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: DW World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T06:38:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Russian media reports say it has numerous military formations made up of former Ukrainian soldiers Image: Kaskad Advertisement
Pavlo Oliynyk, 25, says he joined enemy forces to escape Russian captivity . Before his capture&#160;in the Donbas region in 2024, he was a senior lieutenant for Ukraine 's armed forces.
As a prisoner of war (POW), he says he was electrocuted, beaten, and psychologically tortured. So when he received the offer to join the Maksym Kryvonis battalion —&#160;named after a famous 17th-century Cossack commander —&#160;he accepted.
Oliynyk says he wanted to use this opportunity to reach the front lines, so he could escape to Ukrainian-controlled territory. DW was able to contact Oliynyk through his lawyer.
Reports of Russian combat units consisting of Ukrainian prisoners of war and former servicemembers of Ukraine's armed forces began emerging in late 2022. Several detachments were initially formed and in August 2026, they were merged into the so-called "Ukrainian Volunteer Special Forces Brigade."
Of the individual units, the Maksym Kryvonis battalion has perhaps drawn the most public attention for having evidently taken part in combat. Its true size is unknown —&#160; Russian media reports claim about 200 fighters while available footage only suggests several dozen.
An investigative team of the Ukrainian public broadcaster Suspilne has identified &#160;over 30 members and confirmed they are all former Ukrainian servicemen, captured by Russian forces between 2023 and 2024.
Pavlo Oliynyk says he defected to Russian forces in order to escape captivity Image: Privatarchiv
An 'imitation' of Russian legions fighting for Ukraine
Pavel Luzin, an expert for Russian foreign policy and defense at&#160;the Center for European Policy Analysis, believes these units are an "imitation" of the Russian Volunteer Corps and the Freedom of Russia Legion — units in the Ukrainian armed forces, consisting of Russian citizens — and an attempt by Moscow to distract from the fact that these units exist in the first place. Ukrainian intelligence services tend to agree.
"It bothers them&#160;that Russians would fight&#160;on Ukraine's side, but Ukrainians won't fight on Russia's side," Yevhen Shybalov, author and veteran of the war in Ukraine, tells DW. He adds that Russian troops have never tried to hide the fact that Russian volunteer units in Ukrainian ranks were the main reason Russian forces wanted to create Ukrainian units for their owns ranks.
In 2025, the Office of the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights (OHCHR) reported that 95% of the released Ukrainian POWs interviewed reported torture or ill-treatment during Russian captivity.
Bill Wiley, executive director of the NGO Commission for International Justice and Accountability (CIJA), says that the physical and psychological state of the returned combatants raises serious doubts that their recruitment was voluntary. Mykhailo Savva of the Center for Civil Liberties, a Kyiv-based human rights organization,&#160;points to the criminal procedures running against several of the battalion members, which he says indicates&#160;that these suggest&#160;the combatants had switched sides voluntarily.
But where to draw the line between voluntary and involuntary recruitment? The war veteran and human rights activist Maksym Butkevych, who spent 28 months as a prisoner of war, says the conditions in Russian captivity make that line hard to distinguish. On the one hand, he says, there is a constant atmosphere of pressure, and on the other hand, it's not implausible that some prisoners defected out of ideological proximity.
How were Ukrainian prisoners made to join Russian forces?
Butkevych tells DW that between 2022 and 2023, all Ukrainian prisoners of war had received offers, "more or less persistently," to join the Russian army. In his case, he recounts, it was after he had been severely mistreated. "They showed us they could do anything they wanted to us," the veteran explains, "which was why we should agree to everything."
The veteran Shybalov reports similar experiences. He speaks of attempts to force confessions of war crimes, or to recruit members for Russia's intelligence services, and tells DW all prisoners were first interrogated and then made certain propositions under duress.
"You can torture people to get them to confess, but there comes the difficult moment when you need to give them a weapon," he says. "That's why it's more about psychological pressure, and forcing voluntary compliance."
Butkevych confirms these accounts with his own tales. But neither veteran has ever met someone willing to join Russian units, they say.
Oliynyk, from the Maksym Kryvonis battalion, says he initially turned down multiple bids to join Russian forces. He says he only accepted the fourth offer so he could flee.
He claims he didn't sign official Defense Ministry paperwork, but a private contract with an individual offering military services. Afterwards, he says, he was brought to a room where he heard lectures and saw videos and photos he was later asked to retell.
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
He says fellow servicemembers in the Maksym Kryvonis battalion consisted both of those who had switched sides of their own volition, and those who had been forced.
In a video circulating online that appears to be of a trial in Ukraine, he also says that former Ukrainian POWs in the unit were followed and guarded by mercenaries of the private military company Redut. In the same video, he also speaks of beatings and torture, and receiving monthly wages in envelopes handed out by a member of Russia's military intelligence.
He tells DW he had been given a phone, but that he was too scared to call anyone in Ukraine because it was under constant surveillance. He denies having fought against Ukrainians, as he fled&#160;in March 2025&#160;before his first deployment in the Donetsk province. After running across a field, he says he spent two days hiding from drones in basements and houses before making contact with Ukrainian fighters.
Now, Oliynyk has been in detention in Ukraine for nearly two years while authorities investigate possible treason. He has yet to be formally charged.
Sergey Nikitin contributed to this article, which originally appeared in Ukrainian.

### 30. ★★★☆☆ Ben Roberts-Smith attends Canberra event honouring Australian troops who served in Afghanistan

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T05:27:24+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Accused war criminal Ben Roberts-Smith was among the crowd at the Australian War Memorial on Sunday. Photograph: Lukas Coch/AAP
Accused war criminal Ben Roberts-Smith was among the crowd at the Australian War Memorial on Sunday. Photograph: Lukas Coch/AAP
Ben Roberts-Smith attends Canberra event honouring Australian troops who served in Afghanistan
Anthony Albanese speaks at Australian War Memorial event commemorating 25th anniversary of country’s involvement in the conflict
Get our breaking news email , free app or daily news podcast
Anthony Albanese has honoured Australian troops who served in Afghanistan, using the 25th anniversary of the country’s involvement in the conflict to describe their heroism as a fight for “who we are as a people”.
The prime minister joined hundreds of veterans, family members and dignitaries at a commemoration of the start of the campaign in Canberra on Sunday, with nation’s most decorated living soldier and accused war criminal Ben Roberts-Smith among the crowd at the Australian War Memorial.
“In New York, fires were still burning at Ground Zero from the September 11 attacks a month earlier. The world felt upended,” Albanese said of the start of Operation Slipper.
Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email “Though it would change so devastatingly a year later in Bali, it was not yet directly our fight.
“Yet Australia did what is so resolutely in our nature. We went to support our friend and ally.
“We went because we saw what was at stake, and we went on the principle that September 11 was an attack on a way of life that belonged just as much to us.”
He said the more than 35,000 defence force members deployed over the following two decades did so because the very freedoms “that are at the heart of who we are as a people and a nation were under threat”.
Roberts-Smith, who denies the allegations against him, attended the event alongside family members and supporters, as well as former prime ministers John Howard, Tony Abbott and Scott Morrison.
The governor general, Sam Mostyn, and the opposition leader, Angus Taylor, were also present in the bright sunshine.
One Nation leader Pauline Hanson tells rally Ben Roberts-Smith is a person ‘I respect and I admire’
Roberts-Smith was awarded Australia’s highest military honour – the Victoria Cross – for his service in Afghanistan, but now faces accusations he murdered five unarmed detainees during his deployment.
The former Special Air Service regiment corporal sat alongside his partner, Sarah Matulin, after a judge agreed to vary his bail conditions to allow him to visit Canberra .
Deployments to the Middle East continued until withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021.
US troops left a few months later after signing an agreement with the Taliban, who swiftly returned to power and reintroduced authoritarian control, including strict suppression of women and girls’ rights.
Some 47 Australian personnel died as a result of their service and 263 were wounded, while thousands more allied soldiers were killed.
Tens of thousands of civilians were also estimated to have been killed during the war.
Despite the controversy over Australia’s military involvement in Afghanistan and its legacy, the troops who served on the ground remain intensely proud of what they achieved.
Soldier Mathew Bell was deployed in 2012, aged 24, with his infantry battalion to a forward operating base in Afghanistan’s “badlands” west of Tarinkot.
More than a decade after he returned to Australia, Bell remembers his unit’s work with a sense of honour and accomplishment.
“We changed that little part of Afghanistan, that little part of the world for good,” the former lance corporal, now 38, said.
Bell said girls were able to attend school, medical services were rolled out to villages and “there was freedom”.
“Not the oppression we’re seeing now under the Taliban government,” the father of two said.
“Our role was essentially to go out on either vehicle-mounted patrols or foot patrols, out among the local population through the desert, through the green zone, keeping the Taliban at bay.
“It was a hot area. Once you stepped over that line in the sand, like, that was Taliban territory.”
One of Bell’s close mates was badly wounded in a roadside bomb explosion and on more than one occasion, his section was pinned down by sniper fire.
Services were also held at the Anzac Memorial in Sydney’s Hyde Park, at Melbourne’s Shrine of Remembrance and other sites around the country.
Australians who served in the Afghanistan War were deployed in various roles and missions contributing to reconstruction efforts, democratic governance, training and humanitarian aid.
Operation Slipper was followed between 2015 and 2021 by Operation Highroad and the Kabul airlift in August 2021.

### 33. ★★★☆☆ Taiz hospitals near collapse as Houthis tighten siege: Yemen government

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T03:41:58+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share Taiz hospitals near collapse as Houthis tighten siege: Yemen government on social media
People shop at a market in Yemen's southwestern city of Taiz on October 8, 2026 [AFP]
Yemen’s internationally recognised government has warned of an “imminent health and humanitarian catastrophe” in the besieged city of Taiz, in the country’s southwest, as the Houthis continue their siege and “obstruct essential supplies”.
Yemen’s Ministry of Public Health and Population said in a statement on Saturday that medicines, intravenous fluids, medical supplies and fuel at the city’s health facilities were “nearing depletion”.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 How hunger is devastating Yemen’s children
list 2 of 3 Yemen’s Taiz residents fear food and fuel crisis amid fighting
list 3 of 3 More than 200,000 displaced as fighting escalates in Yemen, UN says
It said emergency care, surgery, intensive care, dialysis and neonatal services could be forced to stop if the situation continued, putting patients, injured people, pregnant women and newborns “at immediate risk”.
Fuel shortages also threaten electricity generators, ambulances and the cold chains that keep medicines and vaccines usable, which could cause “severe complications and preventable deaths”, the ministry said.
It demanded the siege be lifted immediately, and supply routes reopened, with safe passage for patients, ambulances, health workers and aid.
It called on the United Nations and humanitarian organisations to “deliver medicines, intravenous fluids, medical supplies, oxygen and fuel” to the city’s health facilities.
“Every delay increases the risk of service interruption and loss of life,” the ministry said.
The Houthis, an armed group backed by Iran and also known as Ansar Allah, seized Yemen’s capital, Sanaa, by force in 2014 and later imposed their own government. They control the country’s north and northeast.
Taiz endured a devastating Houthi siege between 2015 and 2016, when Yemeni government forces were confined to an enclave in the city centre.
The siege was broken in 2016, though the Houthis kept a partial blockade through other access points. In 2024, they agreed to reopen a route from the east, but it has been closed after fighting resumed.
The Houthis launched an offensive along the Red Sea coast of the Taiz governorate in September, capturing the port city of Mocha, and by October 4 had taken the town of At Turbah, cutting the last main road between Taiz city and other government-held areas.
Yemeni government forces, backed by Saudi Arabia, began a counteroffensive in early October.
Nabil Jamil, director-general of the Yemeni government’s planning and development office in the Taiz governorate, told Al Jazeera the current siege is “more comprehensive and its impact more severe than in 2015”.
He said about 2.8 million people live in government-held areas of the governorate, along with 200,000 who have recently been displaced.
Fuel has become scarce, and on the black market, the price of a 20-litre (5.2-gallon) container of petrol has risen from 30,000 Yemeni riyals ($19) to 90,000 ($58).
“We are still waiting for a response or intervention on the ground,” Jamil said.
The UN refugee agency and the World Health Organization have warned that the fighting is putting Yemen’s fragile health system in peril.
“Civilians must never be turned into a means of exerting pressure in any conflict,” said Nabil Sharyan, a lawyer and head of the Justice for Rights and Development Organization.

### 34. ★★★☆☆ US weighs strikes on Houthis and Iran after deadly Saudi airport attack - Iran International

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Guardian World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T04:19:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: A third attack this week on Riyadh’s international airport on Saturday left at least 12 people dead and more than 300 wounded, Saudi authorities said. Photograph: Yahya Arhab/EPA
A third attack this week on Riyadh’s international airport on Saturday left at least 12 people dead and more than 300 wounded, Saudi authorities said. Photograph: Yahya Arhab/EPA
Twelve killed and hundreds injured in Houthi attack at Riyadh airport
Donald Trump evaluating whether US should join Saudi strikes against the Iran-backed Yemeni rebels
The latest attack on the international airport in Saudi Arabia’s capital of Riyadh killed 12 people and injured more than 300 others, civil aviation authorities said early on Sunday, with some in a critical condition. A Saudi-led coalition blamed Iran-backed Houthi rebels in neighbouring Yemen.
A missile struck a King Khalid international airport complex housing terminals 3 and 4 for domestic flights, a regional official said, speaking on condition of anonymity because they weren’t authorised to speak to the media.
A Saudi statement said the dead included four Saudi citizens as well as two Bangladeshi nationals, a Palestinian, a US citizen, an Egyptian, a Jordanian, a Syrian and a Sudanese. It said the injured were being treated at several hospitals.
The US president, Donald Trump, said he was evaluating whether the US should join Saudi strikes against the Iran-backed Houthi rebels in neighbouring Yemen . “We may. We’re going to look at it,” Trump said. “We just found out about the recent attack. So we’ll make a decision. We move very quickly.”
The US secretary of ⁠state, Marco ⁠Rubio, called the attacks “​reprehensible” and said “they must ​stop ‌now”.
One witness at the Riyadh airport reported hearing an explosion while waiting to board a flight, and another said they saw a strike that left people wounded.
“I was on gate 403,” at the domestic terminal, the witness told the Agence France-Presse news agency. “I saw [gate] 401 hit, I saw injured [people] in front of my eyes. Everyone was running.”
The relative of one injured person said they had been wounded in the shoulders and arms by shattered glass and taken to hospital.
Yemen civil war: what sparked latest escalation and who is involved?
A projectile also fell near the King Fahd international airport in Dammam, according to the Saudi-led coalition fighting the Houthis . The Dammam airport has become an important alternative to Riyadh amid the recent spate of attacks.
There was no immediate statement from the rebels, who earlier Saturday accused Saudi Arabia of striking Sana’a international airport in the Houthi-held Yemeni capital.
Schools in Riyadh will move to remote learning for a week, according to the education ministry, following the deadly attack.
Saturday’s incident comes amid an escalation of missile strikes by Yemen’s Iran-aligned Houthis ⁠on the kingdom. The airport has already been attacked twice this ​week, leaving four people dead and ‌others wounded, and ‌badly damaging an aircraft.
The Houthis have repeatedly attacked Saudi airports, oil facilities and tankers after a truce in their long-running war with Yemen’s Riyadh-backed government unravelled in July.
The attack came as forces allied to the UN-recognised government in Yemen claim to have mounted a successful offensive to retake the Bab al-Mandab strait from Houthi rebels, but accounts vary as to whether the push has been repelled.
Fighters from the Southern Giants Brigades clash with Houthis near the Bab al-Mandab strait in Taiz province, 6 October. Photograph: Yemen Giants Brigades Media Centre/AFP/Getty Images A Houthi spokesperson said forces from the Saudi-backed government based in Aden “were targeted with a large number of ballistic missiles, artillery support and heavy weapons, resulting in the destruction of more than 22 armoured vehicles and the killing and wounding of dozens, including a number of commanders.
“More than 30 ambulances arrived in Aden to transport the dead and wounded from the Saudi enemy’s mobilisations.”
By contrast, the Saudi ambassador to Yemen, Mohammed al-Jaber, said: “Blessed Friday and victory for every Yemeni and Arab. Bab al-Mandab has been cleared of the remnants of the Houthi terrorist militia.”
The Southern Giants, linked to the STC, are the chief pro-government force fighting around the Bab al-Mandab strait. Photograph: Yemen Giants Brigades Media Centre/AFP/Getty Images The exposed 5 sq mile (13 sq km) Perim Island straddles the strait, acting as a lookout point for all traffic from the Red Sea into the Gulf of Aden and towards the Indian Ocean. Aidarous al-Zubaidi, the president of the Southern Transitional Council (STC), one of the main political forces in the south of Yemen, claimed the island and coastal area had been recaptured from Houthi hands.
An official military spokesperson for the UN-recognised government forces qualified the claim, saying only half the area had been recaptured so far. Waddah al-Dabish said newly established Houthi defensive lines had been broken on the land next to the strait, leading to the death of thousands of Houthi fighters.
It is thought that as many as 500 Houthi fighters are stationed on the island, and Saudi Arabia is desperate to be able to report that the strait has been reclaimed to show that the land counteroffensive it launched at the beginning of the week is advancing.
Southern Giants fighters fire at Houthis near the Bab al-Mandab strait in Taiz province, 6 October. Photograph: Yemen Giants Brigades Media Centre/AFP/Getty Images The report came as the United Nations’ special envoy to Yemen, Hans Grundberg, admitted to the UN security council in New York that Yemen – one of the poorest countries in the world – had lapsed back into full-scale war and said the first victims would be women and children.
The Houthis seized control of the strait two weeks ago and said no Saudi-linked commercial traffic would be permitted to go through the narrow waterway. The Houthis are waging a war to remove Saudi influence from Yemen, and have been mounting missile attacks on Riyadh in support of this objective.
There have been previous premature reports of military breakthroughs by the Saudi-backed Yemeni government since it launched its counteroffensive at the start of the week, but the recapture of the land by the strait and Perim Island (also known as Mayyun Island) is a military prize western governments desperately need.
