### 35. ★★★☆☆ Top Chinese US Expert: China Could Cut Purchases from the US by 10 Times the Value of US Arms Sales to Taiwan - Geopolitechs

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN China / DW World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T11:00:57+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Top Chinese US Expert: China Could Cut Purchases from the US by 10 Times the Value of US Arms Sales to Taiwan
Professor Wu Xinbo’s exclusive interview with Phoenix News
At their September summit, the Chinese and US presidents agreed to build a “constructive relationship of strategic stability based on respect, fairness and reciprocity.” This new understanding is gradually taking shape through arrangements in trade, artificial intelligence and security.
On September 28, China announced the outcomes of the eighth round of China-US economic and trade consultations. The arrangements for mechanisms such as the China-US Trade Council and Investment Council were further clarified, while the framework for reciprocal tariff reductions worth $30 billion was developed in greater detail. The two sides also agreed to establish an AI dialogue mechanism, with the next exchange planned for November. Their militaries agreed to work towards a memorandum of understanding on crisis communication and prevention as soon as possible.
Against this backdrop, can Washington’s cautious approach to Taiwan develop into more binding commitments? How will the “irreplaceability” of Chinese supply chains shape future China-US economic relations? Can cooperation on AI move beyond technological competition into applications and risk governance? And with the US midterm elections approaching, what new variables might emerge in Trump’s China policy?
In this exclusive interview, Phoenix Global Insight speaks with Professor Wu Xinbo, Dean of the Institute of International Studies and Director of the Center for American Studies at Fudan University.
1. The September China-US summit added “respect, fairness and reciprocity” to the concept of a “constructive relationship of strategic stability.” This formulation grew out of the principles of equality, respect and mutual benefit that guided earlier trade negotiations. Principles originally applied to economic relations are now being extended to the overall bilateral relationship, providing a clearer framework for cooperation, competition and the management of differences.
2. On Taiwan, Wu argues that China should raise the costs for the United States when Washington takes actions that undermine China’s interests. Whether the US adopts “strategic clarity” or “strategic ambiguity,” China should retain the strategic initiative.
3. On trade, China’s “irreplaceability” extends across the entire supply chain. For the United States, talking about “decoupling” or “de-risking” is much easier than actually achieving it. Wu expects US economic policy towards China to become more pragmatic and targeted, reducing dependence in areas considered sensitive to national security where alternatives are available, while maintaining trade in less sensitive sectors.
4. On AI, China and the United States have considerable room for cooperation beyond technological competition, including applications, commercialisation and risk governance. Wu sees the “zero-sum mentality” of American policymakers as the main obstacle. In AI safety, he argues, cooperation is not a matter of choice but a necessity. However, the involvement of numerous non-state actors makes AI risk governance particularly difficult.
5. On regional conflicts, Wu warns that US secondary sanctions targeting Chinese financial institutions could have the effect of a “financial nuclear bomb,” with consequences for the international position of the dollar. He believes Washington would have to weigh these risks carefully. He also sees opportunities for China-US cooperation on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, Ukraine and the Korean Peninsula.
6. With the midterm elections approaching, Trump still needs a “success story” in his relationship with China. Progress on trade and other bilateral issues would allow him to present diplomatic achievements to American voters. However, changes in US domestic politics could increase pressure over values, Chinese investment and Taiwan.
Interview by: Song Dongze, Chief International Affairs Writer, Phoenix News
Where Did the Six Words “Respect, Fairness and Reciprocity” Come From?
Phoenix Global Insight: The latest China-US summit has expanded the meaning of a “constructive relationship of strategic stability” by adding the principles of “respect, fairness and reciprocity.” When the two presidents agreed in May to build a constructive relationship of strategic stability, you described it as a shift from “tactical stability” to “strategic stability.” How do you assess this latest development? What does it mean for China-US relations going forward?
Wu Xinbo: The principles of “respect, fairness and reciprocity” come from the six Chinese characters meaning “equality, respect and mutual benefit” that were put forward during the China-US trade negotiations. China essentially conducted those negotiations in accordance with these principles. That is why the talks made progress and why the economic relationship stabilised. Now, however, the two sides need to stabilise not only their economic relationship but the entire China-US relationship. During Trump’s visit to China in May, the two countries reached an understanding on building a constructive relationship of strategic stability. The logic behind the latest formulation is the same. If equality, respect and mutual benefit are necessary to stabilise economic relations, then the same principles should apply to the broader bilateral relationship. There has been a slight adjustment in the wording. “Equality, respect and mutual benefit” has become “respect, fairness and reciprocity.” After the May meeting in Beijing, the White House actually used the terms “fairness” and “reciprocity” in its statement. These terms were consistent with the principles China had advocated during the negotiations. The English word “reciprocity” can be translated into Chinese as either “对等” or “互惠.” The nuances are slightly different, but the underlying meaning is broadly similar. So I believe the new six-character formulation draws directly on the principles China followed during the trade negotiations. After discussions between the two sides, these principles have now been extended to the overall China-US relationship in pursuit of strategic stability. We should also recognise that the United States attaches considerable importance to such concepts and formulations. During his welcoming remarks at the White House, Trump specifically mentioned that he and President Xi had established a “great friendship based on mutual respect and the fundamental interests of the two peoples.” So the American side is also talking about mutual respect and the fundamental interests of both peoples. That naturally brings in questions of fairness and reciprocity. These six words therefore reflect the latest China-US understanding on building a constructive relationship of strategic stability.
Phoenix Global Insight: You previously argued that, under the framework of constructive strategic stability, Washington has adopted a more cautious approach to Taiwan than in the past, which has helped maintain overall stability. With the addition of “respect, fairness and reciprocity,” do you think this American caution over Taiwan could develop into more binding policy commitments?
Wu Xinbo: I think the United States must move in that direction, for two reasons. The first is not really about the principles themselves, because principles alone cannot constrain the Americans. What matters more is that President Xi clearly explained China’s position on Taiwan during his visit to the United States. He made three main points. First, China’s position on safeguarding national reunification and territorial integrity is very clear. We are pursuing national reunification. Second, China hopes the United States will maintain what we regard as the correct position of opposing “Taiwan independence.” Since Trump’s visit to China, he has been relatively cautious in handling the Taiwan issue. Third, properly handling Taiwan is a solid foundation for China-US strategic cooperation and the development of bilateral relations. In other words, if the Taiwan issue is not handled properly, there can be no strategic stability between China and the United States, nor can bilateral relations and cooperation develop. That is a clear message about the consequences. The second reason is that China has cards to play. If the United States retreats from its position on Taiwan and damages China’s core interests, it will suffer substantial losses in its relationship with China. So when we talk about “respect” in the phrase “respect, fairness and reciprocity,” the first requirement is respect for China’s core interests, especially on Taiwan. Before President Xi’s visit to the United States, Ambassador Xie Feng published an article in People’s Daily discussing China’s four red lines in China-US relations. President Xi had previously raised these with Biden as well. They concern Taiwan, democracy and human rights, China’s political system and development path, and China’s right to development. I therefore believe these six words provide a clearer framework for how China and the United States should cooperate, the limits of their competition, and how their differences should be managed. They cover all these dimensions.
Whether Washington Chooses “Strategic Ambiguity” or “Strategic Clarity,” China Holds the Initiative
Phoenix Global Insight: Let us turn to specific issues concerning the Taiwan Strait. US arms sales to Taiwan have long been an important factor affecting China-US relations. Around Trump’s visit to China in May, he described a proposed arms sale to Taiwan worth approximately $14 billion as a potential bargaining chip in negotiations with China. US officials subsequently claimed that the sale would not depend on China’s actions. This once again highlights the difficulty of resolving the issue. Diplomatic protests have often failed to prevent Washington from continuing arms sales. How should China respond to prevent the United States from treating arms sales to Taiwan as bargaining chips?
Wu Xinbo: In the past, our responses have generally included diplomatic protests, sanctions against certain American companies and individuals, and military exercises around the Taiwan Strait to exert pressure. But it now appears that these measures have not been strong enough. They have not imposed costs on the United States that it genuinely feels. I think we need to increase the economic and security costs for the United States when it takes such actions. For example, if the United States sells $1 billion or $2 billion worth of weapons to Taiwan, China could cancel procurement contracts with American companies worth two or five times the value of those arms sales. That would impose a real economic cost on the United States.
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond arms sales, the broader direction of US strategy towards Taiwan has also attracted considerable attention. In recent years, some voices in the United States have called for abandoning so-called “strategic ambiguity.” Meanwhile, Washington has continued arms sales to Taiwan, promoted joint development of unmanned systems through the National Defense Authorization Act, deepened military technology cooperation, and considered expanding Taiwan’s participation in regional military exchanges. Does this suggest that the United States is gradually abandoning strategic ambiguity? What implications could such a shift have for the Taiwan Strait?
Wu Xinbo: I don’t think we should be overly concerned about whether the United States adopts strategic clarity or strategic ambiguity. We should not allow ourselves to be led around by Washington. The key to the Taiwan issue is that China must retain the initiative and control over its own decisions. What does that mean? It means having sufficient strength and determination to handle and resolve the Taiwan issue in the way we consider most appropriate. The United States should have to adjust its policies according to China’s choices, rather than China’s Taiwan policy being determined by whether Washington chooses strategic clarity or ambiguity. Look at Biden. During his four years in office, he stated on four occasions that the United States would defend Taiwan if a conflict broke out. That sounded like strategic clarity. Trump no longer says that. He has returned to strategic ambiguity. But Biden’s strategic clarity was not necessarily all that clear, and Trump’s strategic ambiguity may not be all that ambiguous. What do I mean? Biden said the United States would defend Taiwan. But if a conflict actually broke out, would Washington really intervene? Could it win? Could it afford the costs? Trump, meanwhile, refuses to say whether the United States would intervene. But I believe he understands very well that American intervention in a cross-strait conflict would have little prospect of success and would come at an enormous cost. So I don’t think we should pay too much attention to whether Washington calls its policy clear or ambiguous. The important thing is that the Taiwan issue remains in China’s hands. We must retain the initiative.
Phoenix Global Insight: Taiwan’s semiconductor industry is also becoming an increasingly important factor in China-US competition. On September 2, Taiwan announced that local companies planned to invest an additional $20 billion in the United States. Previously, TSMC had expanded its planned US investment to $265 billion. Under pressure from Washington, Taiwan’s advanced semiconductor industry is becoming more deeply integrated into US supply chains. The United States is deliberately linking technological competition with the Taiwan issue. How might this affect the Taiwan Strait and China-US relations?
Wu Xinbo: I think the American motivation is quite straightforward. The United States is worried that a conflict in the Taiwan Strait could disrupt the reliability of its semiconductor supplies. So its main concern is supply security. In effect, Washington is saying: you cannot simply manufacture chips in Taiwan and sell them to us. You need to manufacture them in the United States so that we can feel secure. Now, Taiwan may believe that by cooperating with Washington, building factories in the United States and addressing American supply-chain concerns, it is offering the United States a valuable gift. Taiwan may think this will bring it closer to Washington and encourage the United States to provide stronger support. But I think the outcome could be exactly the opposite. Traditionally, the United States has valued Taiwan partly for geopolitical reasons. But today, particularly for Trump, the main concern is advanced semiconductors. If Taiwanese companies can produce enough advanced chips in the United States to meet American demand, then Washington no longer needs to worry so much about risks in the Taiwan Strait. That would substantially reduce Taiwan’s value to the United States. Taiwan originally hoped that investing in semiconductor manufacturing in America would secure greater American protection. But the result could be that Taiwan actually reduces its own strategic value. Trump might simply think: as long as my chip supply is secure, who cares what happens to Taiwan? So Taiwan could end up making a costly miscalculation. It may try to gain an advantage but end up worse off.
Decoupling Is Easy to Talk About but Difficult to Achieve: China’s “Irreplaceability” Goes Far Beyond Expectations
Phoenix Global Insight: Moving from semiconductors to the broader China-US economic relationship, you previously argued that a country’s resilience in economic competition cannot be measured by trade volumes alone. We also need to consider how difficult it is to replace that country in critical sectors. Where do you think China’s “irreplaceability” is most evident today?
Wu Xinbo: I think China’s irreplaceability can be found in many areas, across almost the entire supply chain, from upstream to midstream and downstream industries. At the upstream level, people immediately think of rare earths, critical minerals and active pharmaceutical ingredients. These are areas where the United States depends on Chinese supply chains. But there are also large numbers of intermediate products that the United States cannot do without. Take America’s AI infrastructure expansion. The United States is building thousands of data centres. Those facilities require all kinds of computer equipment and electrical systems, many of which have to be imported from China. So the United States remains dependent on China. The same is true in advanced manufacturing, including robotics and drones. I would say China’s irreplaceability goes far beyond what many of us imagine. And the United States is becoming increasingly aware of this reality, sometimes painfully so.
Phoenix Global Insight: Despite Washington’s efforts to remove China from its supply chains, bilateral trade has not moved towards complete decoupling. How do you think this situation will evolve? What changes might we see in the economic relationship?
Wu Xinbo: I think slogans such as decoupling and de-risking are much easier to proclaim than to implement. As I just explained, the United States is discovering that it depends on China across almost every part of the supply chain. So it cannot completely decouple, nor can it fully remove China from its supply chains. I therefore expect future US economic policy towards China to become increasingly pragmatic. Washington will need to redefine the boundaries of its economic relationship with China. What does that mean? There will be areas where the United States believes it must reduce dependence on China for so-called national security reasons, and where it actually has the ability to do so. There will be other areas where Washington wants to reduce dependence but cannot realistically achieve it. In those cases, it will have to compromise and find some kind of middle ground. And there will be non-sensitive sectors, unrelated to military conflict, where decoupling is unnecessary and the risks are limited. Trade and economic cooperation can continue in those areas. So I think US economic policy towards China will have to become more pragmatic and more precisely targeted. It will need to establish new boundaries for the relationship.
Phoenix Global Insight: So China-US economic relations may evolve into a state of partial decoupling?
Wu Xinbo: During the Biden administration, the US Secretary of Commerce visited China and made a remark in Shanghai. She said that only about 1% of China-US trade was problematic, while the other 99% was not. So if decoupling is necessary, perhaps only 1% truly needs to be decoupled, and only in areas where the United States can actually achieve it. The remaining 98% or 99% does not need to be decoupled. That gives us a clearer boundary and helps redefine how the two countries should manage their economic relationship. This is extremely important for the stability and predictability of China-US trade.
The Biggest Obstacle to China-US AI Cooperation Is America’s “Zero-Sum Mentality”
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond trade, technology has become increasingly important in China-US relations. Competition over advanced semiconductors and AI continues to intensify. The repeated adjustments to US export policies for Nvidia AI chips illustrate Washington’s dilemma: it wants to restrict China’s access to advanced computing power, but it cannot completely ignore the commercial interests of American companies. As technological competition intensifies, is there still room for limited China-US cooperation on AI? Or will broader strategic rivalry make meaningful cooperation increasingly difficult?
Wu Xinbo: If we look at the situation objectively, China and the United States are already cooperating in AI, and they need to cooperate more in the future. The problem is that American policymakers largely approach the development of the Chinese and American AI industries with a zero-sum mentality. They believe that the United States must not only maintain its technological lead but also do everything possible to suppress China and slow down the development of its AI industry, both technologically and commercially. As a result, the cooperation that already exists between the two countries has not received the recognition it deserves, and the potential for further cooperation has not been fully realised. If we look at the possibilities for cooperation, AI can broadly be divided into three areas: technological development, applications and commercialisation, and governance and risk management. China and the United States are already cooperating in all three areas. Take technological development. The United States is ahead in AI technology, but China has also made important contributions. For example, more and more American AI companies are using models developed by Chinese AI companies. Many of these models are open-source and relatively inexpensive, so an increasing number of American businesses are choosing to use them. On the application side, I think we will see even more opportunities for cooperation. China has moved quickly in applying AI, particularly in robotics and advanced manufacturing. These are areas where China has strengths that the United States needs. Then there is governance. The development and application of AI will bring many risks. As two of the world’s leading countries in AI, China and the United States must discuss what risks these technologies create, how to identify them, how to manage them, and how to cooperate in addressing them. So there is actually enormous room for China-US cooperation in AI. The main problem is that American policymakers are too narrow-minded. They constantly think that maintaining America’s lead requires suppressing China’s development. As a result, they ignore both the reality of existing cooperation and the need for further cooperation. That, in my view, is the biggest obstacle.
Phoenix Global Insight: You just mentioned that China and the United States have room to cooperate in preventing AI risks. Some observers believe the two countries already share certain concerns about extreme AI risks. For example, neither wants powerful AI systems to be misused by terrorist organisations or other malicious actors. Could preventing such risks provide an entry point for China-US cooperation on AI safety?
Wu Xinbo: I think addressing AI risks is precisely where China and the United States need to cooperate. This is not a question of whether they want to cooperate. They have to cooperate. AI development is bringing many disruptive challenges, and humanity has not yet fully understood the risks involved. As more potential risks emerge, the issue will require increasing attention from the international community, especially China and the United States. AI risks also have some unique characteristics. The actors involved may not be states at all. They could be individuals, organisations or companies. That makes the risks much harder to control, because there are simply too many non-state actors. And AI is still at a very early stage of widespread application. We do not yet know how many different kinds of harm, including potentially disruptive or catastrophic consequences, could emerge in the future. There is still an enormous amount of work to be done. That is why governing AI risks will be an extremely complex and difficult task.
The US Must Bear the Consequences of Secondary Sanctions Against Iran; Regional Issues Could Offer Opportunities for China-US Cooperation
Phoenix Global Insight: Looking at the broader international situation, regional conflicts such as the Russia-Ukraine war and the US-Iran conflict are also affecting China-US relations. On August 24, the United States announced an “economic isolation campaign” against Iran, introducing secondary sanctions that could affect third-party markets, including mainland China and Hong Kong. At the same time, US sanctions against Russia continue to affect normal economic and trade relations between China and Russia. For China, this means that even ordinary commercial activities face growing exposure to secondary sanctions. How should China protect its legitimate economic relationships without becoming constrained by the US sanctions system? And how might the spillover effects of these sanctions reshape China’s broader external economic strategy?
Wu Xinbo: Let’s take the US-Iran conflict as an example. The United States wants to intensify sanctions and has even threatened to take action against China. I think China’s position should have two elements. First, we should oppose American sanctions and make clear to Washington that these measures are unjustified and have no basis in international law. On Iran, during the Obama administration, an agreement was reached with the support of China and other countries. It was the United States that subsequently withdrew from that agreement unilaterally, violating international law. Then, last year and again this year, the United States unilaterally launched military attacks against Iran. So, in my view, the United States has violated international law and acted as an aggressor. It is neither legally nor morally justified in its approach to Iran. Under these circumstances, its sanctions are unreasonable and cannot be accepted by the international community. The second issue concerns the consequences if Washington targets Chinese financial institutions or major state-owned banks. We need to explain very clearly to the United States what such actions would mean. China is the world’s largest trading nation. A large share of our international trade is settled in US dollars, and we also hold hundreds of billions of dollars in US debt securities. China therefore plays an important role in supporting the international position of the dollar and the market for US government debt. If the United States sanctions Chinese banks, that would be like detonating a financial nuclear bomb. And if the result is an acceleration of China’s move away from the dollar, the United States will have to bear the consequences. China is a major economic, trading and financial power. If an economy of China’s size begins moving away from the dollar, Washington had better think carefully about the implications. We need to make these consequences clear to the Americans. As I said earlier, Americans know how to calculate costs and benefits. They understand what is at stake.
Phoenix Global Insight: Beyond the economic consequences of sanctions, regional conflicts such as the Russia-Ukraine war and the US-Iran conflict continue to affect global security. Although China and the United States disagree on some of these issues, they still share interests in preventing conflicts from spiralling out of control and maintaining energy and shipping security. Could the two countries cooperate in practical ways on these issues?
Wu Xinbo: I think the most urgent issue right now is the US-Iran conflict and the situation in the Middle East. The question of passage through the Strait of Hormuz affects global energy supplies and the direction of the world economy. Is there room for China-US cooperation here? Of course there is. For China, reopening the Strait of Hormuz and restoring freedom of navigation serve our interests, as well as those of the overwhelming majority of countries around the world. So we want freedom of navigation through the Strait to be restored as soon as possible. On the one hand, China can work with Iran. We should make clear that the Strait of Hormuz must not be held hostage to the US-Iran conflict. It is an international waterway, and disruptions there have consequences far beyond the countries directly involved. On the other hand, we also need to work with the United States. We should tell Washington that launching unilateral military attacks against Iran was wrong, that it needs to acknowledge its mistake and correct its policies. That means not launching further attacks and not continuing economic sanctions. If compensation is necessary, it should provide compensation. If guarantees against future military action are needed, it should provide those guarantees. Only then can Iran develop sufficient trust in the United States to reach a new agreement. So I think China should engage with both sides. We need to work with Iran, but even more importantly, we need to work with the United States. That is how China can play a constructive role.
Phoenix Global Insight: Apart from Iran, which other regional issues might provide opportunities for limited China-US cooperation?
Wu Xinbo: I think the Russia-Ukraine conflict and the Korean Peninsula are both areas where China and the United States could cooperate. On Ukraine, Trump has launched another round of diplomatic efforts, and the parties involved in the negotiations have sent some positive signals. If the directly involved parties become willing to sit down and negotiate, China could work with other countries to play a constructive role. Any eventual settlement of the Russia-Ukraine conflict is unlikely to be purely bilateral. It would probably need to be a multilateral agreement, with the United States, China and other major countries potentially acting as guarantors. In that sense, it could resemble the Iran nuclear agreement reached during the Obama administration. China could certainly make a constructive contribution to such a process. The Korean Peninsula is another example. We can see that South Korea’s Lee Jae-myung administration wants to improve inter-Korean relations and advance the peace process on the peninsula. Trump has also repeatedly expressed interest in meeting the North Korean leader to discuss improving US-North Korea relations. China could use its friendly relations with North Korea to play a positive and constructive role, including facilitating communication and offering proposals for the parties to consider. So I believe there are many international and regional issues on which China and the United States could cooperate. In the past, particularly during Trump’s first term, his foreign policy agenda was rather narrow. When it came to China, he was mainly interested in trade. In his second term, fentanyl was added to the agenda. But now it appears there are more issues than that. With the Iran situation, the United States seems to want China’s help in resolving problems. China could also play a role in Ukraine. As two major powers, China and the United States naturally have extensive opportunities for cooperation in international and regional affairs. If we can continue expanding cooperation on third-party issues through leader-level diplomacy, we will broaden the areas of cooperation in the bilateral relationship. That would create additional positive momentum for the stability and development of China-US relations.
With the Midterm Elections Approaching, Trump Wants to Turn China-US Relations into a Foreign Policy Success Story
Phoenix Global Insight: Finally, let’s turn from regional conflicts back to American domestic politics. The United States will hold its midterm elections this November. As the elections approach, how much room does Trump still have to adjust his China policy and negotiate with Beijing?
Wu Xinbo: I think Trump still depends heavily on presenting his China policy as a success. What he wants to say is: look how well I have handled the relationship with China. This is one of my foreign policy achievements. So he needs not only to stabilise the relationship but also to demonstrate progress. For example, achievements in trade or cooperation on drug enforcement are things he can present to American voters as evidence of his success.
Phoenix Global Insight: If the Democrats regain control of Congress after the midterm elections, how much could that affect Trump’s China policy during the second half of his second term? And looking further ahead, how might it shape US policy towards China after Trump leaves office?
Wu Xinbo: If the Democrats take control of the House of Representatives, I think the impact on China-US relations could be felt in three main areas. The first concerns values. Democrats may criticise Trump for treating relations with China primarily as a matter of doing business, while paying insufficient attention to human rights, democracy and values. They may argue that these issues must be given greater priority. So they could play the values card, creating additional political pressure. The second area concerns Chinese investment in the United States. Trump may consider using the proposed China-US Investment Council to encourage more Chinese investment in America. From his perspective, this would be good for the US economy. Chinese investment could create jobs and generate tax revenue. But Democrats, partly because of their ties to organised labour, may be concerned about Chinese investment, particularly in the American automotive industry. We have already seen Democratic lawmakers write to Trump urging him not to open the US automobile market to Chinese manufacturers. During the Biden administration, the United States imposed 100% tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles to prevent them from entering the American market. Democrats may therefore demand much stricter scrutiny and restrictions on Chinese investment. That is the second possible impact. The third area is Taiwan. Since the May summit between the Chinese and American presidents, Trump has generally handled Taiwan rather cautiously, including on arms sales. If the Democrats return to power in Congress, they may argue that improving relations with mainland China must not come at Taiwan’s expense. They may invoke the Taiwan Relations Act and argue that the United States is obligated to provide Taiwan with weapons. They could therefore press Trump to approve further arms sales. That would also affect how the Taiwan issue is handled. There may be other areas of impact, but I think these three would be the main ones.
Phoenix Global Insight: How much influence can Congress actually exert over Trump? After all, his willingness to disregard Congress seems almost unprecedented.
Wu Xinbo: Foreign policy is largely the responsibility of the executive branch, particularly the president. Congress can make noise and apply pressure, but ultimately Trump makes the decisions. What concerns me is the possibility that, after the midterm elections, the Democrats return to power and intensify their political confrontation with Trump. That could make Trump even more dependent on support from his Republican colleagues in Congress. And there are quite a few pro-Taiwan figures within the Republican Party. They might say to him: “Mr President, we can support you, but you need to sell Taiwan some weapons. It has been quite a while since the last major sale.” Trump may not necessarily listen to what Democrats say. But when Republicans make such demands, he may find it much harder to ignore them. That is one possible scenario. In other words, pressure from the Democrats could ultimately be transmitted through the Republican Party.
Phoenix Global Insight: Thank you, Professor Wu Xinbo, for this in-depth discussion, which has helped clarify the dynamics of China-US competition and cooperation across the Taiwan Strait, trade, technology and global governance.

### 36. ★★★☆☆ Can Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (NYSE:TSM) Justify A Premium On Cash Flow? - Simply Wall Street

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T06:37:58+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing has ridden the AI chip wave into the center of the global semiconductor conversation, and the current share price puts fresh focus on one thing: Are the cash flows that TSMC can realistically generate over time enough to underpin where the stock trades today?
Over the past 3 years, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing has returned about 417.0%, which puts a very large gain on the table that investors now need to weigh against the durability of its cash generation.
Record recent revenue tied to demand for advanced AI chips, plus heavy capital spending on new fabs and packaging, can reshape both the level and timing of the company’s future free cash flows.
If you&#x27;d rather focus on earnings, this one&#x27;s for you. See why Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing&#x27;s 29.8x P/E tells a different valuation story .
The issue now is whether the intrinsic value implied by Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing’s cash flows, as estimated by a Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) approach, lines up with the price investors are paying in the market.
If you are evaluating Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing based on its cash flows and AI exposure, it may be useful to compare it with other AI infrastructure plays using 92 AI infrastructure stocks .
Does Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Look Pricey on Cash Flow?
The Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) model here uses a two stage Free Cash Flow to Equity approach based on Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing’s projected cash generation. Latest twelve month free cash flow sits around NT$1.1t, and the model assumes that this cash base keeps growing rather than shrinking over the next decade as major projects move from heavy build out toward fuller utilisation.
Those projections ramp free cash flow into the multi trillion NT$ range over time. However, the DCF outcome still points to an intrinsic value meaningfully below the current share price of $453.31. Record 2026 revenue tied to AI chips and the huge NT$ level capex program help explain why the market is willing to pay up today, because investors appear to be giving strong weight to long term AI demand even though the cash flow model is more restrained. - **发生了什么**: Broadcom (NasdaqGS:AVGO) is leading a planned US$50b debt financing package for OpenAI&#x27;s Nexus custom AI chip buildout, disclosed on 9 October 2026.
The financing push targets large scale AI infrastructure, as OpenAI seeks dedicated chip capacity beyond general purpose GPU supply.
Sector peers such as Oracle and SpaceX are also pursuing multibillion dollar funding deals to secure next generation chip manufacturing and data center resources.
Broadcom&#x27;s role in organising US$50b of debt for OpenAI&#x27;s Nexus chips is only part of the funding story now reshaping AI credit markets. We have also spotted 1 warning sign worth knowing about at Broadcom.
To put this Broadcom move in context, explore other AI infrastructure plays through 92 AI infrastructure stocks .
NasdaqGS:AVGO 1-Year Stock Price Chart Broadcom designs and supplies semiconductor hardware and infrastructure software, which puts it directly in the flow of capital rushing into AI data centers and custom chip projects like Nexus. Its role as a large US firm with a US$1.7b market cap helps it act as a coordination point for complex financing tied to that buildout.
See how Broadcom&#x27;s balance sheet measures up .
Broadcom takes on more AI financing risk to deepen revenue visibility
For Broadcom, organizing US$50b of debt for OpenAI’s Nexus chips leans into the existing narrative that AI revenue visibility is strengthening while financing risk is becoming the real test. The move supports the bull case catalyst of multi year AI semiconductor demand from hyperscalers and reinforces the idea that Broadcom is central to large custom XPU deployments. At the same time, it adds weight to the bear side that already flags AI XPV structures and off balance sheet exposure as key risks, because another large financing layer ties Broadcom more tightly to a small group of AI customers and to residual value assumptions on high cost hardware.
See how these catalysts shape Broadcom&#x27;s path to a $532 fair value .
The clearest signpost from here is how Broadcom frames its AI XPV commitments and residual value guarantees at the next detailed quarterly report. This includes any updated figures on maximum exposure versus free cash flow and whether consolidated gross margin guidance reflects the added mix of financed, lower margin AI XPUs.

### 40. ★★★☆☆ Microsoft's Nadella says AI needs an ‘emergency brake’ that humans control

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T20:59:28+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Microsoft&#x27;s Nadella says AI needs an ‘emergency brake’ humans control
Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said advanced AI systems should include containment, independent controls and an "emergency brake."
Other AI and tech industry leaders have called for stronger safeguards and, in several cases, pacing frontier development.
President Donald Trump opposes slowing U.S. AI development, while his administration has launched a safety-focused task force.
Satya Nadella, chief executive officer of Microsoft Corp., during an event in San Francisco, California, US, on Wednesday, Oct. 7, 2026.
Microsoft Chairman and CEO Satya Nadella on Saturday called for advanced artificial intelligence systems to be built with containment, independent controls and an "emergency brake" that lets authorized people pause or shut a model down mid-task.
"We need to surround non-deterministic models with strong, deterministic system design, human controls, and reliable operating procedures, and establish industry standards where existing ones are insufficient," Nadella wrote in a post on social media platform X.
"Treating frontier closed and open weight models like insider risks is a way to build such a system," he said.
Nadella&#x27;s comments come amid warnings from top tech executives and researchers — including Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates , Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei , OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk — about insufficient AI safety protocols and claims that the technology is moving too fast.
An AI researcher quit Anthropic last month and accused the company, and its chief rival, OpenAI, of "gambling with our lives." Later that day, an alignment lead at Anthropic focused on AI safety said there&#x27;s a greater than 10% chance that the technology could "kill all humans" within the next decade.
Conversely, President Donald Trump has repeatedly dismissed AI extinction risks and has instead emphasized that the industry needs to stay ahead of China. Trump also recently introduced a new "AI Force," led by Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton , to help facilitate the industry and root out bad actors.
Microsoft&#x27;s Nadella wrote Saturday that AI systems should be designed around "principles of observability."
They include model diversity; a human-readable footprint of the model&#x27;s actions; continuous system testing; independent controls and auditability; containment; and incident disclosure, Nadella wrote.
"The most trustworthy Super Intelligence system will not be the one with the model we trust most," he wrote. "It will be the one that enables us to trust the model the least."
Choose CNBC as your preferred source on Google and never miss a moment from the most trusted name in business news.

### 42. ★★☆☆☆ Fed Minutes Offer a Guide to Future Interest Rate Moves. First, You Must Decode Them. - Barron's

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T18:45:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Expectations of Another Fed Rate Hike This Month Are Shifting Dramatically
Expectations of a second interest rate hike by the Federal Reserve at its Oct. 27-28 meeting are suddenly plummeting. As recently as Sept. 28, the futures market put the probability of a rate hike this month at 71%. Today, futures traders are pricing in just a 20% probability that the Fed will hike. What gives?
Image source: Getty Images. Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again. In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. For the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. Continue »
Well, first of all, the September employment report, published on Oct. 2, was much weaker than expected. Economists expected the economy to add at least 84,000 new jobs during the month, yet the actual figure was just 29,000. In addition, the unemployment rate ticked up a notch, from 4.1% to 4.2%.
That suggests the labor market is weakening. So the Fed, which has a mandate to maximize employment, might be more reluctant to raise rates, a move that can slow the economy and undermine job creation.
Also, there are signs that inflation , which has been above the Fed's target level for several years, might be cooling a bit. The personal consumption expenditures price index, which is the Fed's preferred measure of consumer inflation, rose 3% year over year, when food and energy prices are excluded. That was down from 3.3% the previous month.
And annual wage growth slowed slightly. Wage growth is an important aspect of inflation, and one that the Fed monitors very closely.
Finally, the Fed itself is sounding more dovish. New York Federal Reserve President John Williams said in a Sept. 29 speech that "there is no need for urgency" on the next rate hike and that the Fed has time to gather more information about what's going on in the economy.
So, the Fed appears to be taking a very gradual approach to raising its target interest rate. That's very good for the stock market , as research on how the market reacts to rate hikes has shown that the pace of a hiking cycle matters a lot. One study by the brokerage Charles Schwab looked at rate hikes going back to 1946 and found that when the Fed moves quickly on hikes, the market is down, on average, 16.5% 12 months after the first hike. When it goes slowly, the market drawdown shrinks to 12.2%.
Schwab says it doesn't expect an aggressive tightening campaign by the Federal Reserve, and I agree. I've monitored all of Fed Chair Kevin Warsh's comments since he took the helm at the central bank, as well as some of his colleagues, and it all suggests that the Warsh-led Fed wants to move gradually and deliberately, closely monitoring all the economic data on inflation, economic growth, and the labor market along the way.
Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again
In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. If you'd invested $5,000 then, you'd be sitting on $2,980,404 today.*
Now, for the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. It's a key player in the $1.8 trillion space race, and with the stock recently sitting 20% off its highs, the window to get in early is closing fast.
Charles Schwab is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Matthew Benjamin has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Charles Schwab and recommends the following options: short September 2026 $95 calls on Charles Schwab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy .
Expectations of Another Fed Rate Hike This Month Are Shifting Dramatically was originally published by The Motley Fool

- **发生了什么**: A drone attack by Sudan&#x27;s paramilitary forces has killed 41 people in the country&#x27;s Blue Nile region bordering Ethiopia, a rights group has said.
The strike by the Rapid Support Forces (RSF) targeted a camp for displaced people in the state&#x27;s Abu Shandi area on Friday, Emergency Lawyers said.
The UN Office for the Co-ordination of Humanitarian Affairs (Ocha) said it was "appalled by horrifying reports". It said initial reports suggested most of the casualties were women and children.
The Sudanese army and paramilitary forces including the RSF have been locked in a power struggle since 2023, a conflict that has killed hundreds of thousands and displaced more than 14 million from their homes.
It has also created one of the worst humanitarian crises in the world, with millions of people needing medical help.
The army controls much of Sudan&#x27;s central and eastern regions, while the RSF is headquartered in the western region of Darfur and also controls areas in the south.
Both sides have been accused of using drone attacks.
Ocha said in a statement that the area reportedly struck on Friday had been "under sustained atttack in recent weeks, displacing thousands of civilians and exposing many more to grave harm".

### 45. ★★☆☆☆ Delhi braces for more protests as 'cockroach' group's leaders released

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T07:10:49+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Image caption, Reports said 7,000 people were detained on Saturday and released late that night
By Geeta Pandey Reporting from BBC Correspondent, Delhi
A court in the Indian capital has ordered the release of top leaders of the Gen Z-led Cockroach Janta Party (CJP) even as the city braces for protests for a second day with thousands of security personnel deployed and roads barricaded.
Abhijeet Dipke - who began the movement in May after India&#x27;s Chief Justice likened some jobless young people to "cockroaches" - and other leaders had been detained on arrival in Delhi on Saturday to lead protests seeking electoral reforms.
Police refused them permission to protest and unprecedented curbs were in place to thwart the protesters from reaching the venue.
But thousands did try - media reports said 7,000 were detained and released late at night.
The CJP - which is not registered as a political party in India - is seeking electoral reforms just months before crucial state elections.
It has also demanded the resignation of Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) Gyanesh Kumar over allegations of manipulating voters&#x27; lists - which the Election Commission has denied.
On Sunday morning, the CJP lawyer Ratna Singh said she had been informed by the police that Dipke and two other senior CJP leaders - Saurav Das and Ashutosh Ranka - will be produced before the magistrate by the police seeking their remand "to maintain public peace".
She later posted on X that the three had been set free by the court "on the condition that they will not engage in breach of peace for one year" - it&#x27;s not clear what that means and whether the CJP leaders can take part in protests.
Saturday saw dramatic developments as police officials boarded the flight to detain Dipke, Das and Ranka as their plane landed.
Figure caption, &#x27;Cockroach&#x27; leaders say they have been detained ahead of march
Police had refused permission for the protest to take place at Jantar Mantar, a landmark in the Indian capital, and the city was in a virtual lockdown to prevent the protesters from reaching the venue.
At least 45 metro stations were shut and Delhi&#x27;s main railway stations were shut with major train services cancelled. Many protesters attempting to travel from outside were de-boarded en route.
Much of Delhi looked like a city under siege on Saturday, with roads barricaded and offices, shops and restaurants shut.
The day began with expectations of a major gathering at Jantar Mantar, but only a handful of protesters managed to reach the site before police swiftly removed them.
The road that the CJP had occupied for weeks earlier this summer seeking education reforms remained barricaded at both ends, guarded by thousands of police and paramilitary personnel.
The deployment continued through the day, but little happened at the site.
Elsewhere in the city, thousands of protesters still turned up, including activists and senior lawyers who were all detained.
Student leader Neha Bora tried to march from Dilli Haat to Jantar Mantar but was also stopped and taken away. They were all released later on Saturday.
One woman who managed to evade the police and reach Connaught Place told us: "In a democracy, everyone has the right to protest and to say if we are not happy.
"Peaceful protests have been there for ages. We are not living in pre-independent India. We request the government to let us protest."
Figure caption, Watch: Why have hundreds of &#x27;cockroach&#x27; protesters been detained in Delhi
The row centres over a mammoth exercise to update electoral rolls nationwide - the commission has said it&#x27;s a routine exercise to weed out dead, duplicate or ineligible voters.
At least 130 million voter names have been purged from the rolls so far and in many states, the exercise has been shrouded in controversy, with tens of thousands of people alleging they have been wrongly removed.
The CJP - and opposition parties - accuse Kumar of favouring Prime Minister Narendra Modi&#x27;s Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) in the way the electoral rolls are being updated.
The Election Commission and the BJP have denied this.
The CJP first emerged as a major protest movement during weeks of demonstrations over the summer demanding education reforms and the resignation of Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan.
The group stemmed from a joke made by Dipke in response to a remark by Chief Justice Surya Kant, who had likened jobless youngsters to "cockroaches". He later said the comments were misinterpreted.
Dipke, who previously worked with the opposition Aam Aadmi Party (AAP), had just completed a master&#x27;s degree at Boston University when he posted a question on X in May: "What if all cockroaches come together?"
The post went viral, and the CJP was born. Momentum built around the movement which tapped into anger over unemployment, exam paper leaks and government accountability.
Their five-week-long protest ended in celebration for the protesters in July after the resignation of India&#x27;s education minister.
Meet India&#x27;s feisty Gen Z protester who is taking on Modi
Lockdown-like scenes at usually bustling central Delhi markets
India&#x27;s &#x27;cockroach&#x27; protesters are back - this time asking poll chief to resign

### 47. ★★☆☆☆ Candidate named Hitler Mussolini elected mayor in Peru

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T12:00:10+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Benito Mussolini with Adolf Hitler at a meeting in Florence, Italy, in 1940. Photograph: Everett Collection Historical/Alamy
Benito Mussolini with Adolf Hitler at a meeting in Florence, Italy, in 1940. Photograph: Everett Collection Historical/Alamy
Candidate named Hitler Mussolini elected mayor in Peru
Leftwing Hitler Mussolini Simeon Flores beats far-right candidate in election in remote district of Andes
A businessman called Hitler Mussolini Simeon Flores has been elected the mayor of a remote district in the Peruvian Andes, according to local election data.
The 45-year-old, who shares two of his names with the notorious European dictators, won the race for mayor of Queropalca, in Peru’s central Huánuco region, and will take office on 1 January.
The candidate from the leftwing Ahora Nación party won 229 out of a total of 663 votes in elections on 4 October, according to the national office of electoral processes.
His closest rival, the far-right candidate Gaspar Osorio, received 204 votes.
Flores, who works in freight transportation, had belonged to Popular Force, the party of Peru’s rightwing president, Keiko Fujimori, until 2025, according to electoral authorities.
Despite the name being associated with Germany’s Nazi leader Adolf Hitler, several candidates with the name have won elections in Peru.
In 2021, the doctor Hitler Saavedra Casternoque was elected to parliament for the centrist party Somos Peru.
In 2018, Hitler Alba Sánchez was voted in as mayor of the Andean district of Yungar, saying during his campaign that he was “the good Hitler”.

### 48. ★★☆☆☆ ‘You have a meeting’: the calendar phishing scam growing exponentially

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T06:00:18+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Calendar apps can add invitations automatically. Photograph: grinvalds/Getty Images
Calendar apps can add invitations automatically. Photograph: grinvalds/Getty Images
‘You have a meeting’: the calendar phishing scam growing exponentially
Appointment or renewal reminder appears in victims’ calendar to lure them into logging in to fake Google, Microsoft or PayPal page
Y ou’re preparing for the week ahead and take a look at your Google calendar. There’s an entry for a meeting that you must have completely forgotten. The note that pops up when you click on it says you’ll be reviewing a project and includes a link to more details. Confused, you follow it to find out more; the website you land on asks for logon details, and you provide them.
Unfortunately, this was not a failure of your memory but a calendar phishing scam. You have handed your name and password to fraudsters who will sell it on in a batch with other people’s details, use it to break into your work email, or to persuade you that they are contacting you from your bank , or another institution.
Luke Wescott, a threat detection engineer at Sublime Security, says calendar phishing is still relatively new but the company has seen “exponential growth”.
The scam takes several forms, including a fake meeting, or a prompt to renew a service you pay for. In all, an entry appears in your electronic calendar.
It ends up there after scammers send an email to your work, or personal address, with a calendar request. It doesn’t matter if you miss it, or it ends up in your spam folder.
Fake notifications of an auto-renewal are common to persuade people to respond and hand over personal details. Photograph: Sublime Security “Calendar apps, such as Google Calendar, can add invitations automatically without users even accepting them,” Wescott says. “So scammers don’t even need you to open an email.”
And while you may not look twice at one from someone whose name you do not recognise, when something turns up in your calendar you may treat it differently. “It can create a borrowed credibility by showing up in the same place as your dentist appointment or a weekly 1:1 with your boss,” he says.
Max Gannon, an intelligence analysis manager at the cybersecurity company Cofense, says some scammers are using legitimate platforms such as Zoom to send the invitations, making them look more convincing to the recipients, and to security software.
“That makes it really hard to block,” he says. “Even AI-backed blockers struggle.”
If you set up something to filter out invitations from these platforms, he says “that would also block any legitimate meeting invitations”.
You will receive an email and see an entry in your calendar. The titles vary: examples seen by the Guardian include fake meetings, notifications of voicemail messages, and software renewals.
Wescott says this is standard. “We typically see stuff like ‘New voicemail received’, ‘Payment receipt confirmation – $298.99’, ‘PayPal unusual activity’ warnings, ‘Your auto-payment will be processed within 24 hours’, and invitations to bid on a contract,” he says.
Sometimes the calendar phishing scam uses fake voicemail notifications. Photograph: Supplied Gannon says the invitation could look like it has come from within your company. “If they want to they could make it look like an internal invitation,” he says. “They can customise the content, may be adding the logo of your company.”
The event description will typically contain a link, or a phone number.
Wescott adds: “Either they want you to click a link to a fake login page like Microsoft, Google or PayPal, and enter your password, or to call a ‘support’ number to cancel a charge that never actually happened.”
Until you click on this, or make a call, you are not compromised. The scammers do not have access to everything in your calendar – they are just trying to lure you to a place where you will hand over your details.
As Wescott says, “it sounds familiar to typical phishing, it’s the same technique, just using a different delivery method”.
Don’t panic if you see an unexpected meeting or reminder in your calendar. Treat it as you would an unexpected email and don’t click on the link.
“My number one advice is people just need to be paranoid,” Gannon says. “It doesn’t matter if the invitation has come from someone two desks down, you’ve got to be suspicious of everything.”
Wescott advises turning off automatic accepting. In Google Calendar you can go into settings and opt to only accept invitations from known senders, or when you have accepted.
He adds: “Don’t click ‘decline’ on suspicious invites as it can tell the sender that your address is ‘live’. Instead, delete or report it as spam/junk.”
- **为什么重要**: 含假设 / 演习 / 推测成分，实际落地前影响力有限，已降权。

### 51. ★★☆☆☆ Has Yemen returned to ‘full-scale war’? - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T14:14:13+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Share Has Yemen returned to ‘full-scale war’? on social media
A makeshift camp near Aden in Yemen for people displaced by clashes between Iran-aligned Houthi fighters and Saudi-backed Yemeni government forces, October 7, 2026 [Reuters]
Fighting continues to intensify in Yemen as the internationally recognised government forces push a military offensive against the Iran-backed Houthis, an armed group also known as Ansar Allah, which controls part of the country.
Speaking at the United Nations Security Council on Friday, the UN Special Envoy to Yemen, Hans Grundberg, said the country has effectively slid back into “full-scale war”.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 UN envoy warns Yemen has returned to ‘full-scale war’, urges dialogue
list 2 of 4 Three killed, 36 injured in Houthi attacks on Saudi airports, Riyadh says
list 3 of 4 Saudi-led coalition says it’s carrying out operation in Yemen
list 4 of 4 Yemeni government forces claim 1,860 Houthis ‘neutralised’
Government forces are particularly looking to retake the important Bab al-Mandeb strait at the entrance to the Red Sea, a vital trading chokepoint that has become an important alternative to the Strait of Hormuz, where Iranian attacks have all but halted shipping.
At least 46 civilians have been killed and more than 130 others injured since August, many of them children, according to the United Nations.
More than 200,000 people have been displaced since fighting escalated in September, the UN’s migration agency (IOM), said.
Fierce battles across several fronts have prompted concerns from rights groups that Yemen has essentially returned to civil war after the relative quiet brought by a 2022 ceasefire that followed a lengthy civil war which first erupted in 2014.
Amid the US-Iran war, Yemen’s worst fighting in years threatens to destabilise the region further, experts say. Here’s what we know:
Houthi rebels march with a ‘boycott American and Israeli goods’ placard during a mobilisation campaign in Sanaa, September 10, 2026 [AP] What’s the latest on the ground?
Saudi-backed Yemeni forces are advancing on the Houthis in a bid to recapture areas around the Bab al-Mandeb, a critical trade route for global shipping into and out of the Red Sea. Riyadh has increasingly relied on it for oil exports amid the US-Iran war, which has closed the Strait of Hormuz.
On Sunday, the Yemeni government announced the start of the operation “Dawn of Yemen”, which aims to retake all remaining Houthi-held territory, including the capital, Sanaa.
On Saturday, it said it had conducted more than 2,750 precision strikes during military operations over the previous 24 hours, resulting in the “neutralisation” of more than 1,400 Houthi fighters.
The spokesperson for the armed forces, Colonel Majid al-Nuzaili, said in a statement carried by Saba news agency that the operations also targeted high-value military assets deep within Houthi-held territory.
He added that ground forces “successfully” breached Houthi defences, recaptured operationally significant positions, secured their field gains and continued to advance the offensive according to operational plans.
The Bab al-Mandeb strait emerged as yet another front in the US-Israel war on Iran when the Houthis declared a blockade on ships linked to Saudi Arabia in July.
While the Houthis said that was in retaliation for ongoing Saudi blockades on Yemen, analysts viewed the move as calculated to provide more leverage for Iran amid on-and-off negotiations with the US – a close ally of Riyadh.
[Al Jazeera] Battles are now raging around the strategic southern cities of Mocha and Dubhab, which are on the Red Sea coast and strategically important for control of Bab al-Mandeb. The Houthis, who have controlled Sanaa and parts of northwestern Yemen since 2014, seized the area in September, effectively taking control of the strait, although both sides currently claim control of the shipping lanes.
The Houthis, who have developed sophisticated weapons systems, including ballistic missiles, have also targeted Saudi Arabia with missile and drone attacks. On Friday, Saudi Arabia’s aviation authorities said three Saudi citizens, including a pilot, had been killed and several others wounded in Houthi strikes on the international airport in Riyadh – the first such deaths in the kingdom since fighting resumed. Several airlines suspended flights.
The coalition forces also heavily bombarded Sanaa, on Friday, with several injuries reported, including a child. Both sides are also fighting to control Taiz.
Civil war began in 2014 when the Houthis took control of Sanaa and imposed their own government in the capital. The seizure came amid dissatisfaction with the ruling government and following a revolution three years earlier.
A coalition of armed forces and local armed groups backed by Saudi Arabia launched military operations and restored the internationally recognised government in Aden. Fighting resulted in the deaths of between 150,000 and 227,000 people, while millions were displaced.
While the Houthis controlled much of the north, the Yemeni government controlled the southern regions.
A UN-brokered truce in 2022 saw a lull in fighting and allowed aid to reach the deprived population, which has faced famine since 2016.
In 2023, the Houthis, who form part of Iran’s “Axis of Resistance” alliance of proxy armed groups, began disrupting shipping in the Red Sea in response to Israel’s genocidal war on the Gaza Strip. In March this year, the Houthis fired missiles at Israel amid the war on Iran, officially entering the US-Israel war with Iran which began in February.
Tensions escalated when the group declared a naval blockade of Saudi ports and shipping passing through the Bab al-Mandeb strait in July, shattering the relative calm in the country since 2022. It followed the announcement with a lightning ground offensive on Mocha, pushing back Yemeni coalition forces from the strategic port city.
In September, the group’s forces again swept through and seized territory along the western coast and the Bab al-Mandeb strait, including the strategically located Mayyun Island in the strait.
On October 4, Rashad al-Alimi, leader of the Yemeni government’s Presidential Leadership Council (PLC), announced a military offensive to “restore state authority”.
Analysts say the scale of fighting means Yemen has fallen back into civil war as both sides come under scrutiny for targeting civilians.
As the UN Special Envoy to Yemen, Hans Grundberg, declared that the country has effectively slid back into “full-scale war”, civilians, including women and children, are the ones bearing the “brunt of escalating violence”.
The envoy stressed the need for the UNSC to support urgent mediation once more, although analysts have pointed out that recent gains by Yemeni government forces could mean they are more determined to push through the ongoing offensive, rather than agree to a settlement.
But “unless war is to become Yemen’s permanent reality, the time to consider political strategies is now”, Grundberg warned.
Reporting from Sanaa, Al Jazeera’s Yousef Mawry, said: “It is beginning to resemble the 2015 Saudi-led coalition war on Yemen, when Sanaa and various other provinces were attacked relentlessly around the clock.”
A number of sites in Sanaa have been targeted, he said, including Sanaa International Airport. Several industrial facilities, including a chips factory in the north and a sponge factory in the south, have also been hit.
At least four telecommunications infrastructure sites have been struck in the capital and throughout areas controlled by the Houthi movement. Mount Attan in Sanaa was also hit by 10 air raids.
Mawry reported that, according to Saudi coalition officials, strikes have destroyed 136 military targets in areas under Houthi control, including military sites, launch pads and radars belonging to the Houthi group.
“Meanwhile, the Houthis accuse Saudi Arabia of targeting public infrastructure and telecommunications facilities, warning that the campaign risks plunging the country back into a humanitarian crisis similar to the one experienced from 2015 to 2022,” Mawry said.
The US, which has previously indicated that it does not want to get involved in the Yemen conflict, nevertheless on Friday called on the Houthis to stop attacks on Saudi Arabia and Yemen’s internationally recognised government, while demanding that Iran end its support for the group.
Speaking at a UN Security Council meeting on Yemen, Jeff Bartos, the US ambassador to the UN for Management and Reform, said Washington was “gravely concerned” by the Houthis’ recent escalation, including attacks on civilian infrastructure and airports.
“The pattern remains unmistakable. The Houthis act as instruments of Tehran to threaten freedom of navigation, undermine regional stability, and terrorise innocent civilians,” Bartos said.
“We demand that the Houthis cease their attacks against the legitimate governments of Yemen and Saudi Arabia and we demand that Iran cease all support to the Houthis,” he added.

- **发生了什么**: The rapid growth of the cloud and AI markets is driving natural gas prices higher, since U.S. data centers get over 40% of their electricity from natural gas. That&#x27;s been a boon for natural gas producers and the midstream pipeline companies that transport the gas.
But as winter approaches, natural gas prices will likely climb even higher as temperatures drop and heating costs rise. To profit from that shift, investors should consider investing $2,000 (or more) in these two top pipeline stocks: Energy Transfer ( ET -1.02% ) and The Williams Companies ( WMB +0.46% ) .
Energy Transfer operates more than 140,000 miles of pipeline across 44 states. It transports crude oil, natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGL), and other refined products through its pipelines, and exports natural gas (LNG) through its marine export terminals.
Roughly 30% of all natural gas produced in the U.S. flows through Energy Transfer&#x27;s pipelines. It typically generates around 40% of its adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) from its natural gas assets. That percentage will increase after it closes its $2.6 billion acquisition of Vaquero Midstream later this year.
$70B Market cap calculated using publicly traded shares outstanding only. Does not include unlisted, private, or dual-class non-traded shares. Implied market cap may vary.
Energy Transfer is a master limited partnership ( MLP ), which combines its own income with a return of capital to pay more tax-efficient distributions than regular corporations. That&#x27;s why it pays a high forward yield of 6.6%. Those payments only consumed about half of its distributable cash flow (DCF) over the past few years, so it has plenty of room to raise its payout.
However, investors in MLPs must file an extra K-1 form to report that income every year. If you don&#x27;t mind that extra work, it still looks like a bargain at 12 times this year&#x27;s earnings per unit.
The Williams Companies operates over 32,000 miles of pipeline in 24 states. It&#x27;s smaller than Energy Transfer, but it mainly transports natural gas and NGLs. It owns Transco, the largest natural gas pipeline in the U.S, which connects the Gulf Coast to the Atlantic Seaboard.
$89B Market cap calculated using publicly traded shares outstanding only. Does not include unlisted, private, or dual-class non-traded shares. Implied market cap may vary.
Williams transports about a third of all natural gas produced in the U.S., making it a more focused "pure play" on natural gas than Energy Transfer and many of its industry peers. Williams is also structured as a regular C-corporation rather than an MLP, which might make it more appealing to investors who don&#x27;t want to deal with the extra tax paperwork.
Williams pays a forward dividend yield of 2.9%, which may seem low relative to other pipeline stocks, but its trailing payout ratio of 82% leaves plenty of room for future hikes. It isn&#x27;t a screaming bargain at 27 times forward earnings, but its direct exposure to the booming natural gas market -- without the noise of crude oil -- could justify that higher valuation.

### 53. ★★☆☆☆ DIU publishes data on 20 Russian shadow fleet vessels carrying oil and gas - Ukrinform

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T04:44:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukraine's defense intelligence has published data on 20 vessels involved in exporting Russian crude oil, petroleum products and liquefied natural gas, as well as bunkering sanctioned vessels.
The Defense Intelligence of Ukraine (DIU) reported this on Telegram , according to Ukrinform.
"These vessels are part of the logistics network that the Kremlin uses to generate revenue to finance its genocidal war against Ukraine. All of them are currently under sanctions imposed by the United Kingdom or Ukraine, while one of the vessels is subject to U.S. restrictions," the statement said.
The DIU highlighted Russian vessel PORTOVYY (IMO 9246621) among the main subjects of the publication. The vessel is designed to transport and store liquefied natural gas (LNG).
The agency said that since 2021, the tanker has been owned by sanctioned Russian company PJSC Gazprom and has served as a floating gas storage facility at the Portovaya complex in the Leningrad region.
"On August 15, 2026, PORTOVYY, carrying a cargo of Russian LNG, left the Gulf of Finland for the first time since November 2021. The tanker sailed along the Northern Sea Route to the Chinese port of Beihai for unloading," the agency said.
The vessel is currently at the Zhoushan Huafeng Shipyard on Zhoushan Island in China, which has been designated as the site for its maintenance.
The agency also published details about two new vessels involved in Russia's shadow fleet of gas carriers, which the aggressor state hastily acquired through front companies around the world in spring 2026.
They are the conventional LNG tanker AVACHA (IMO 9319404) and the gas carrier CHAIVO (IMO 9319404).
AVACHA was previously owned by China's Jovo Energy but was acquired in spring 2026 by the little-known company Tudor Maritime Ltd, registered in the Marshall Islands.
Since June 2026, the conventional LNG tanker has sailed under the Russian flag and has been operated by the newly established Russian entity Kryoline Management LLC (TIN 6500028647).
According to the agency, since August 2026 the vessel has been transporting gas from the Utrenny terminal of the sanctioned Arctic LNG 2 project to the Beihai hub in China.
CHAIVO was previously owned by Greece's Maran Gas Maritime but was also acquired by Tudor Maritime Ltd.
"Since July 2026, the vessel has been transferred to the Russian flag and placed under the umbrella of Kryoline Management LLC. Since August this year, the gas carrier has been transporting cargoes to the port of Beihai from the sanctioned non-self-propelled floating gas storage unit KORYAK FSU, located in the waters of the port of Petropavlovsk-Kamchatsky," the DIU said.
The agency explained that, in an effort to preserve critically important revenues from energy exports, Russia is not only attempting to build ice-class vessels at its own shipyards but is also systematically and proactively expanding its shadow fleet through a network of offshore companies.
Read also: UK imposes new sanctions on Russia's oil, shadow fleet , war supply chains
The agency called on foreign partners to coordinate their efforts, including by strengthening controls over the sale of vessels owned by commercial entities, decisively cutting off Russia's energy revenues that are converted into weapons used against civilians, and blocking opportunities for sanctioned vessels to receive services at foreign shipyards.
"Every Russian tanker carrying sanctioned oil or gas is a legitimate target for international sanctions pressure," the agency said.
As reported, the DIU uncovered a scheme used by Russian agricultural tycoons to evade sanctions while generating revenue in the EU to finance the war against Ukraine.
