### 56. ★★☆☆☆ Trump says Zelenskyy ‘better damn well stop' attacks on Russian oil refineries, calls for new Ukrainian leader - Politico

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T18:41:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: President Trump said Saturday that it was time for Ukraine to get a new president, suggesting the current leader, Volodymyr Zelenskyy , has not done enough to end his country's war with Russia.
"I suggest they get a new leader who can make a deal because he could have made many deals and for some reason he never does," Mr. Trump told reporters as he was leaving the White House for a trip to Tennessee.
He also blamed Ukraine's recent strikes on Russian oil refineries for contributing to high diesel prices before the midterm election, despite the ongoing war in Iran .
"He wants to make problems for the world. He wants to make problems for our farmers, our ranchers, with diesel fuel," Mr. Trump said.
Mr. Trump also suggested that if he had been U.S. president in 2022, Russia's invasion of Ukraine "would have never started."
"There was no reason that the war with Ukraine and Russia started," he said. "It started due to incompetence."
Zelenskyy, Mr. Trump said, has "lost 30% of your country and you've had over a million people go between the two of them, over 2 million people killed. That's a war that should have never started. And I think it's time for Ukraine to get a new president."
President Trump speaks to members of the media as he departs the White House on the South Lawn on Oct. 10, 2026, in Washington, DC.
Ukraine's foreign minister said Saturday that Zelenskyy had the backing of the Ukrainian people and deserved support.
"He has the support of the Ukrainian people, and they have the right to decide," Foreign Minister Andriy Sybiga said of Zelenskyy in a post on X. "Statements from abroad only confirm that he is doing his job well. He deserves support."
The American president's comments came hours after Zelenskyy offered pointed criticism of a new diesel deal between the U.S. and Russia. He said the deal would help bankroll Moscow's war in Ukraine.
According to Mr. Trump's announcement on Friday, Russia will immediately supply more than 300,000 tons of diesel, followed by an additional 500,000 tons in November and 1 million tons "immediately thereafter."
"Additionally, based on the condition of their Diesel Refineries, Russia will then deliver, within a short period of time, 3,000,000 Tons of Diesel Fuel," Mr. Trump wrote on Truth Social.
Mr. Trump's shocking announcement was in stark contrast to a sweeping Russia sanctions law he signed last month that aimed at clamping down on the oil and gas revenues that fuel Russia's war against Ukraine.
The Treasury Department announced Friday that it would temporarily waive sanctions on Russia's energy sector to "allow the supply of Russian diesel to the global market."
In a lengthy post online reacting to the deal on Friday, Zelenskyy said Russia would repay it "with further terror and perfidy." He cast it as an investment in the war, allowing it to be prolonged.
"It plays into Russia's hands &mdash; allowing it to kill more, wage war for longer, have even less respect for America, and inflict even greater losses and damage on the world," he said.
Former Vice President Mike Pence blasted the deal on social media , saying it "makes no sense and is a betrayal of the legacy and leadership of the late Senator Lindsey Graham," who was a fierce Ukraine supporter. He added that Mr. Trump should "rescind the deal immediately."
"America should not be throwing a lifeline to Putin's war machine," Pence said in the post. "Now is the time to stand strong against Russian aggression as we did during the first Trump administration. Putin doesn't want peace. Putin wants Ukraine. I have no doubt that if Putin overruns Ukraine, it will only be a matter of time until Russia's military crosses a border that our troops will have to deal with him."
Meanwhile, Russian strikes killed at least 20 people, including three children, in Ukraine's Zaporizhzhia region, officials said on Saturday.
Russian aerial bombs hit residential buildings in Zaporizhzhia, Mykhailo Semikin, the head of the regional military administration, said Saturday. At least 18 people were wounded.
A man talks on the phone while sitting on the rubble of a residential building destroyed by a Russian airstrike in Zaporizhzhia, Ukraine, on Saturday, Oct. 10, 2026.
Missiles also struck Kyiv in a daytime attack Saturday. Mayor Vitalii Klitschko said four people were wounded.
Russia and Ukraine are locked in a long-range aerial war while their armies remain largely deadlocked on the front line, more than 4 1/2 years since Moscow's full-scale invasion. Ukraine is struggling to counter escalating Russian bombardments that have killed more than 60 people over the past four days.
Rescue workers clear the rubble of a residential building destroyed by a Russian airstrike in Zaporizhzhia, Ukraine, on Saturday, Oct. 10, 2026.
"Today, Russia expressed its thanks for the lifting of sanctions on diesel exports by dropping five glide bombs on residential buildings in Zaporizhzhia," Zelenskyy wrote Saturday on X.
He said the attacks show Moscow understands "any easing of pressure to mean they can keep fighting, to mean they can keep dropping bombs on cities," adding that "different signals are needed."
Russian guided missile kills 30 on packed Ukrainian city buses

### 58. ★★☆☆☆ Two Carlton players treated in hospital after off-season night out

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T05:05:45+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Carlton AFL players Lachie Cowan and Will Hayward required treatment in hospital on Sunday morning. Photograph: James Ross/AAP
Carlton AFL players Lachie Cowan and Will Hayward required treatment in hospital on Sunday morning. Photograph: James Ross/AAP
Two Carlton players treated in hospital after off-season night out
Club issues brief statement about Lachie Cowan and Will Hayward, saying latter suffered minor cut to head after a fall
An off-season night out for Carlton football club teammates ended with two players admitted to hospital for treatment on Sunday morning, in what the club says are “separate circumstances”.
The club released a statement on Sunday confirming that AFL players Lachie Cowan, 21, and Will Hayward, 27, had been treated and discharged, after reports in several media outlets about the aftermath of an off-season night out.
Guardian Australia understands both players were both part of a larger group that had been socialising on Saturday evening.
Will Hayward in action in August. Photograph: Rob Prezioso/AAP The club statement said Cowan had “received medical treatment and underwent observation, before being discharged a short time later” but did not provide any more information about the circumstances.
Hayward was also admitted to hospital later on Sunday morning, after returning home.
“Will Hayward experienced a minor cut to the head after a fall, and admitted himself to hospital where he received stitches before being discharged,” Carlton said.
Neither player is understood to be seriously unwell or hurt.
Isaac Heeney ‘can’t wait’ to resume AFL career after suspension for hotel incident
“The club has been in regular contact with both players, with priority for their wellbeing and both players have now received the necessary care relating to their separate circumstances.”
Ambulance Victoria said paramedics responded to an incident at inner-suburban South Yarra at about 5.15am on Sunday.
A man in his 20s was taken by ambulance to the Alfred hospital in stable condition, Ambulance Victoria said.
AFL players throughout the league are on leave as trade period rolls on until Wednesday night.

### 59. ★★☆☆☆ Pauline Hanson seems more and more like the de facto opposition leader. At CPAC, Angus Taylor warmed the seat for her

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-10T19:00:09+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Pauline Hanson speaks during the CPAC conference in Brisbane on Saturday. The One Nation leader signalled her keenness for her party to work with the Liberals to defeat Labor. Photograph: Russell Freeman/AAP
Pauline Hanson speaks during the CPAC conference in Brisbane on Saturday. The One Nation leader signalled her keenness for her party to work with the Liberals to defeat Labor. Photograph: Russell Freeman/AAP
Pauline Hanson seems more and more like the de facto opposition leader. At CPAC, Angus Taylor warmed the seat for her
Cooperation between One Nation and the Liberals ‘up to Angus Taylor’, Hanson says at conservative conference. How would that work?
Get our new political email , free app or daily news podcast
The annual Conservative Political Action Conference billed itself as a chance for the leaders of the three main conservative parties – Liberals, Nationals and One Nation – to make their pitch and compete for rightwing voters.
The reception they received in Brisbane on Saturday suggests Pauline Hanson is effectively the preferred opposition leader, with Angus Taylor relegated to leading a minor party.
But how would they work together, if the opportunity arose? While Hanson repeatedly criticised the Coalition , to laughter from the audience, the One Nation leader is keeping the door open to some kind of arrangement to defeat Labor.
‘Smarmy management consultant’: Angus Taylor criticised by Pauline Hanson and heckled at CPAC event
Discussion of how to “unite the right” echoed around the Brisbane convention centre.
It was always a risk for Taylor, still leader of the second-largest bloc in parliament, to agree to equal billing on the conference lineup with Hanson, who leads a party room of five others. The Liberal leader was the first guest speaker, with his office calling his opening address a “keynote” but CPAC had effectively advertised all three leaders – Taylor, Hanson and Matt Canavan – as co-headliners.
This framing would be remarkable in itself, if Hanson hadn’t already been operating as de facto opposition leader through 2026, while filling a void left by Taylor who has struggled for mainstream cut-through and political oxygen. Hanson followed Taylor on the schedule but it’s the first time in a while – Taylor has been following her lead all year.
That Hanson literally took a seat Taylor had just vacated as they conducted back-to-back interviews with Karl Stefanovic, the One Nation leader nestling herself into a spot Taylor had just warmed up for her, was a moment that felt symbolic.
Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here
Taylor was always up against it, in front of a CPAC crowd which leans more conservative than the centre-right. Beginning with a mea culpa for Liberal governments past – apologising for his role in immigration increases, pandemic spending and net zero – Taylor admitted his side “made mistakes” but were now “unashamedly on the right”. His speech favoured conservative talking points, including “big government”, “climate bureaucracy”, “NDIS fraud”, and no less than six mocking references to “socialism”.
While CPAC usually finds speaking slots for half the Coalition frontbench each year, Taylor is the first sitting Liberal leader to feature. It’s interesting that he felt the need to, or wanted to, front a convention where the demographic is somewhat outside the voters the Coalition must win back – those in the centre.
This audience represents a very activated, politically engaged conservative base. But with the Coalition vote still cratering eight months from Sussan Ley’s dumping, and One Nation’s rise carving off voters on the right, trying to win back some of that base makes sense for the Liberal leader.
It was a valiant effort to reclaim the Coalition’s mantle as “the most credible alternative government” but not even Taylor’s newly announced immigration crackdown – confirmed, coincidentally or otherwise, just days before CPAC – could save him from heckles from those more partial to Hanson. A handful booed as he said net overseas migration would rise from 100,000 to 160,000 under the Coalition plan; more booed when he dodged questions from Stefanovic about why he wasn’t trying harder to work with Hanson.
This is where the problem lies for Taylor, presenting a vexed conundrum with no easy solution. While the Liberal leader’s speech ran through a list of relatively well-explained Coalition policies – cutting taxes, indexing tax brackets, instant asset write-offs – and philosophical markers for how the Coalition would intellectually approach government, it was clear that much of the crowd wasn’t buying what he was selling. The biggest claps Taylor got were when he railed against socialism and big government, not for talking up fiscal discipline.
One Nation’s AI video is vile and tired – but the ensloppification of Australian politics doesn’t stop there
Hanson, by contrast, got a standing ovation from most of the room before even walking on stage, with a hype video – set to Vanessa Amorosi’s Amazing – playing her on. More scripted than usual, Hanson’s speech went to economic pain, anger at climate policies and “mass migration”, and criticism of “elitists”. Nearly every line received enthusiastic applause.
Despite claiming Australians don’t want a “ smarmy management consultant ” replacing Anthony Albanese, and questioning how conservatives could “trust” the Coalition, Hanson repeatedly signalled her keenness for rightwing parties to cooperate in some way.
In a press conference, she wouldn’t say exactly how that would work. Hanson said she didn’t want ministry positions for One Nation in a Coalition government, nor would she accept Coalition ministers in a One Nation government, appearing to rule out a formal agreement.
But asked if One Nation would continue playing hardball and beating up the Liberals, or if cooperation might emerge, she said it was “up to Angus Taylor”.
After seeing how his message went across to the Brisbane convention room, compared with Hanson’s, Taylor may have a lot to think about.
Josh Butler is a Guardian Australia political reporter and chief of staff in Canberra
